Parameter estimation for linear parabolic SPDEs in two space dimensions based on high frequency data
Yozo Tonaki, Yusuke Kaino, Masayuki Uchida

Abstract
. We consider parameter estimation for a linear parabolic second-order stochastic partial differential equation (SPDE) in two space dimensions driven by two types Q Q -Wiener processes based on high frequency data in time and space. We first estimate the parameters which appear in the coordinate process of the SPDE using the minimum contrast estimator based on the thinned data with respect to space, and then construct an approximate coordinate process of the SPDE. Furthermore, we propose estimators of the coefficient parameters of the SPDE utilizing the approximate coordinate process based on the thinned data with respect to time. We also give some simulation results.



1.
Introduction

We consider the following linear parabolic
stochastic partial differential equation (SPDE)
in two space dimensions

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X_{t}^{Q}(y,z)

\displaystyle=\biggl\{\theta_{2}\biggl(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial y^{2}}+\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z^{2}}\biggr)+\theta_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}+\eta_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}+\theta_{0}\biggr\}X_{t}^{Q}(y,z)\mathrm{d}t

(1.1)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sigma\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{Q}(y,z),\quad(t,y,z)\in[0,1]\times D,

\displaystyle X_{0}^{Q}(y,z)

\displaystyle=\xi(y,z),\quad(y,z)\in D,

\displaystyle X_{t}^{Q}(y,z)

\displaystyle=0,\quad(t,y,z)\in[0,1]\times\partial D,

where
D=[0,1]^{2}
,

W_{t}^{Q}
is a
Q
-Wiener process in a Sobolev space on
D
,
an initial value
\xi
is independent of
W_{t}^{Q}
,

\theta=(\theta_{0},\theta_{1},\eta_{1},\theta_{2})
and
\sigma
are unknown parameters
and
(\theta_{0},\theta_{1},\eta_{1},\theta_{2},\sigma)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\times(0,\infty)^{2}
.
Moreover, the parameter space
\Theta
is a compact convex subset of

\mathbb{R}^{3}\times(0,\infty)^{2}
,

\theta^{*}=(\theta_{0}^{*},\theta_{1}^{*},\eta_{1}^{*},\theta_{2}^{*})
and
\sigma^{*}

are true values of the parameters
and we assume that
(\theta^{*},\sigma^{*})\in\mathrm{Int}\,\Theta
.
The data are discrete observations

\{X_{t_{i}}^{Q}(y_{j_{1}},z_{j_{2}})\}
,

i=0,\ldots,N
,
j_{1}=0,\ldots,M_{1}
,
j_{2}=0,\ldots,M_{2}

with
t_{i}=i\Delta_{N}
,
y_{j_{1}}=j_{1}/M_{1}
and
z_{j_{2}}=j_{2}/M_{2}
,
where
\Delta_{N}=1/N
and
M:=M_{1}M_{2}
.

SPDEs appear in various fields
such as physics, engineering, biology, and economics.
In particular, the linear parabolic SPDE is a fundamental equation,
and its typical example is the stochastic heat equation.
The heat equation is known as the equation which describes the heat conduction
in an object and the diffusion phenomenon of particles,
and it is an essential equation which appears in various situations.
For example, in the heat equation with one space dimension,
we can consider the temperature variability of a thin object such as a wire,
or a sea surface on a straight line.
However, it is insufficient to consider practical problems
with only a heat equation in one space dimension.
Actually, in our daily problems,
we often deal with two or three dimensional heat phenomena,
such as temperature variability of a thin steel plate, sea surface, solid, or seawater.
For instance, Piterbarg and Ostrovskii
[
20
]

treated sea surface temperature variability.
Thus, it is important to analyze the SPDEs in two space dimensions
because it can address more general problems than SPDEs in one space dimension.

Statistical inference for SPDE models based on
discrete observations has been studied by many researchers, see, for example,
Markussen
[
19
]
,
Bibinger and Trabs
[
1
]
,
Chong
[
3
]
,
[
2
]
,
Cialenco et al.
[
5
]
,
Cialenco and Huang
[
7
]
,
Hildebrandt
[
9
]
,
Kaino and Uchida
[
13
]

and references therein.
Recently, Kaino and Uchida
[
14
]
proposed
the adaptive maximum likelihood type estimation for
the coefficient parameters of linear parabolic second-order
SPDEs in one space dimension with a small noise.
Hildebrandt and Trabs

[
11
]
,
[
10
]

studied the estimation for the coefficient parameters of
linear parabolic and semilinear SPDEs in one space dimension, respectively,
using a contrast function with double increments in time and space.

In particular,
Bibinger and Trabs
[
1
]

treated the following linear parabolic SPDE in one space dimension

(1.2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X_{t}(y)

\displaystyle=\biggl(\theta_{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial y^{2}}+\theta_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}+\theta_{0}\biggr)X_{t}(y)\mathrm{d}t+\sigma\mathrm{d}B_{t}(y),\quad(t,y)\in[0,T]\times[0,1],

\displaystyle X_{0}(y)

\displaystyle=\xi(y),\quad y\in[0,1],\qquad X_{t}(0)=X_{t}(1)=0,\quad t\in[0,T],

where
T>0
,
B_{t}
is a cylindrical Brownian motion in a Sobolev space on
[0,1]
,

\xi
is an initial value,

\theta_{0},\theta_{1},\theta_{2}
and
\sigma
are unknown parameters
and
(\theta_{0},\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\sigma)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(0,\infty)^{2}
.
They proposed minimum contrast estimators for

\kappa=\theta_{1}/\theta_{2}
,
\sigma_{0}^{2}=\sigma^{2}/\theta_{2}^{1/2}

in the case where
T
is fixed.
Since the coordinate process
x_{k}(t)
,
k\geq 1
of
the SPDE (
1.2
) is expressed as

x_{k}(t)=\sqrt{2}\int_{0}^{1}X_{t}(y)\sin(\pi ky)\mathrm{e}^{\kappa y/2}\mathrm{d}y

and is a diffusion process satisfying the stochastic differential equation

(1.3)

\mathrm{d}x_{k}(t)=-\lambda_{k}x_{k}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\sigma\mathrm{d}w_{k}(t),\quad x_{k}(0)=\sqrt{2}\int_{0}^{1}\xi(y)\sin(\pi ky)\mathrm{e}^{\kappa y/2}\mathrm{d}y

where
\{w_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
is independent real valued standard Brownian motions
and
\lambda_{k}=-\theta_{0}+\frac{\theta_{1}^{2}}{4\theta_{2}}+\pi^{2}k^{2}\theta_{2}
,
Kaino and Uchida
[
13
]
constructed
an approximate coordinate process by a Riemann sum

\hat{x}_{k}(t)=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\sqrt{2}X_{t}(y_{j})\sin(\pi ky_{j})\mathrm{e}^{\hat{\kappa}y_{j}/2}

with observations of space
\{y_{j}\}_{j=1}^{M}
and an estimator
\hat{\kappa}
of
\kappa
,
and proposed estimators of
\sigma^{2}
,
\theta_{2}
and
\theta_{1}

using the approximate coordinate process based on
the thinned data with respect to time
and statistical inference for diffusion processes.
Furthermore, they extended the results of
[
1
]
to the case where

T
is large and proposed estimators of

\sigma^{2}
,
\theta_{2}
,
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{0}
.
For details of statistical inference for diffusion processes
based on discrete observations,
see
Genon-Catalot and Jacod
[
8
]
,
Kessler
[
15
]
,
and
Uchida and Yoshida
[
22
]
,
[
23
]
.

In this paper, we apply
the estimation method based on the approximate coordinate process
to SPDEs in two space dimensions
and consider the estimation for each coefficient parameter of the SPDE (
1.1
).
In this case, we need to be careful in setting a noise
W_{t}^{Q}

because
X_{t}^{Q}
may not be square integrable for any
t>0

depending on the choice of the
Q
-Wiener process.
Moreover, since the method of
[
13
]
is
based on the property that the random filed
X_{t}
admits a spectral decomposition

X_{t}(y)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}x_{k}(t)e_{k}(y)

with
e_{k}(y)=\sqrt{2}\sin(\pi ky)\mathrm{e}^{-\kappa y/2}
and
x_{k}(t)
in (
1.3
),
it is also required to set a noise
W_{t}^{Q}
such that
the random field
X_{t}^{Q}
in the SPDE (
1.1
) can be decomposed.
See Hübner et al.
[
12
]

and Cialenco and Glatt-Holtz
[
6
]

for parameter estimation for SPDEs driven by a
Q
-Wiener process.
See also the surveys by Lototsky
[
17
]
and Cialenco
[
4
]

for statistical inference based on the spectral approach.

This paper is organized as follows.
In Section
2
, we present the setting of our model.
We also discuss how to choose a
Q
-Wiener process
and introduce two types of
Q
-Wiener processes.
In Section
3
, we first propose minimum contrast estimators
of the parameters appearing in the coordinate process of the SPDE
by using the thinned data with respect to space.
Next, we construct an approximate coordinate process
by using these minimum contrast estimators,
and provide estimators of the coefficient parameters of the SPDE
based on the thinned data with respect to time.
We then show that the estimators of the coefficient parameters
are asymptotically normal.
In Section
4
, we give some simulation studies.
Section
5
is devoted to the proofs of our assertions in Section
3
.
In order to understand the characteristics of the parameters
of the SPDE (
1.1
),
the sample paths with different values of the
parameters are provided in Appendix section.

2.
Preliminaries

Let
(\Omega,\mathscr{F},\{{\mathscr{F}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}P)

be a stochastic basis with usual conditions,
and let
\{w_{k,\ell}\}_{k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}}

be independent real valued standard Brownian motions on this basis.

Let
A_{\theta}
be the differential operator defined by

-A_{\theta}=\theta_{2}\biggl(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial y^{2}}+\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z^{2}}\biggr)+\theta_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}+\eta_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}+\theta_{0}.

Note that the SPDE (
1.1
) is represented as

\mathrm{d}X_{t}^{Q}(y,z)=-A_{\theta}X_{t}^{Q}(y,z)\mathrm{d}t+\sigma\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{Q}(y,z).

For
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
the eigenfunctions
e_{k,\ell}
of
A_{\theta}
and
the corresponding eigenvalues
\lambda_{k,\ell}
are given by

\displaystyle e_{k,\ell}(y,z)

\displaystyle=2\sin(\pi ky)\sin(\pi\ell z)\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\theta_{1}}{2\theta_{2}}y}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\eta_{1}}{2\theta_{2}}z},\quad(y,z)\in D,

\displaystyle\lambda_{k,\ell}

\displaystyle=-\theta_{0}+\frac{\theta_{1}^{2}+\eta_{1}^{2}}{4\theta_{2}}+\pi^{2}(k^{2}+\ell^{2})\theta_{2}.

We then obtain that

A_{\theta}e_{k,\ell}=\lambda_{k,\ell}e_{k,\ell}

for
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.
For real valued functions
f
and
g
defined on
D
, let

\langle f,g\rangle_{\theta}=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}f(y,z)g(y,z)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}}y}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\eta_{1}}{\theta_{2}}z}\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}z,\quad\|f\|_{\theta}=\langle f,f\rangle_{\theta}^{1/2}.

Moreover, set

H_{\theta}=\{f:D\to\mathbb{R}|\,\|f\|_{\theta}<\infty\text{ and }f(y,z)=0\text{ for }(y,z)\in\partial D\}.

We consider two types of
Q
-Wiener processes,

\{W_{t}^{Q_{1}}\}_{t\geq 0}
and
\{W_{t}^{Q_{2}}\}_{t\geq 0}
, defined as follows.

(2.1)

\langle W_{t}^{Q_{1}},f\rangle_{\theta}=\sum_{k,\ell\geq 1}\lambda_{k,\ell}^{-\alpha/2}\langle f,e_{k,\ell}\rangle_{\theta}w_{k,\ell}(t),

(2.2)

\langle W_{t}^{Q_{2}},f\rangle_{\theta}=\sum_{k,\ell\geq 1}\mu_{k,\ell}^{-\alpha/2}\langle f,e_{k,\ell}\rangle_{\theta}w_{k,\ell}(t)

for
f\in H_{\theta}
and
t\geq 0
,
where

\mu_{k,\ell}=\pi^{2}(k^{2}+\ell^{2})+\mu_{0}
,
\mu_{0}\in(-2\pi^{2},\infty)

and
\alpha\in(0,1)
.

\mu_{0}
is an unknown parameter (may be known),
the parameter space of
\mu_{0}
is a compact convex subset of

(-2\pi^{2},\infty)
and the true value
\mu_{0}^{*}
belongs to its interior.
The restriction
\alpha>0
is for mathematical reasons,
and the restriction
\alpha<1
is for statistical inferences.
See Remarks
1
,
2
and
4

for details of the
Q
-Wiener processes
and the restriction of
\alpha
.

We assume that
\xi\in H_{\theta}
and

\lambda_{1,1}^{*}=-\theta_{0}^{*}+\frac{(\theta_{1}^{*})^{2}+(\eta_{1}^{*})^{2}}{4\theta_{2}^{*}}+2\pi^{2}\theta_{2}^{*}>0
.

X_{t}^{Q}
is called a mild solution of (
1.1
) on
D

if it satisfies that for any
t\in[0,1]
,

X_{t}^{Q}=\mathrm{e}^{-tA_{\theta}}\xi+\sigma\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{-(t-s)A_{\theta}}\mathrm{d}W_{s}^{Q}\quad\mathrm{a.s.},

where

\mathrm{e}^{-tA_{\theta}}u=\sum_{k,\ell\geq 1}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda_{k,\ell}t}\langle u,e_{k,\ell}\rangle_{\theta}e_{k,\ell}
for
u\in H_{\theta}
.
By defining the
Q_{1}
-Wiener process
W_{t}^{Q_{1}}
in (
2.1
),
the random field
X_{t}^{Q_{1}}(y,z)
is expressed as

(2.3)

X_{t}^{Q_{1}}(y,z)=\sum_{k,\ell\geq 1}x_{k,\ell}^{Q_{1}}(t)e_{k,\ell}(y,z),

where the coordinate process

x_{k,\ell}^{Q_{1}}(t)=\langle X_{t}^{Q_{1}},e_{k,\ell}\rangle_{\theta}=\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda_{k,\ell}t}\langle\xi,e_{k,\ell}\rangle_{\theta}+\sigma\int_{0}^{t}\lambda_{k,\ell}^{-\alpha/2}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda_{k,\ell}(t-s)}\mathrm{d}w_{k,\ell}(s)

is the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process
which satisfies the stochastic differential equation

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}x_{k,\ell}^{Q_{1}}(t)=-\lambda_{k,\ell}x_{k,\ell}^{Q_{1}}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\sigma\lambda_{k,\ell}^{-\alpha/2}\mathrm{d}w_{k,\ell}(t),\quad x_{k,\ell}^{Q_{1}}(0)=\langle\xi,e_{k,\ell}\rangle_{\theta}.

Note that

(2.4)

x_{k,\ell}^{Q_{1}}(t)=2\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}X_{t}^{Q_{1}}(y,z)\sin(\pi ky)\sin(\pi\ell z)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\theta_{1}}{2\theta_{2}}y}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\eta_{1}}{2\theta_{2}}z}\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}z.

Similarly, by defining the
Q_{2}
-Wiener process
W_{t}^{Q_{2}}
in (
2.2
),
the random field
X_{t}^{Q_{2}}(y,z)
is spectrally decomposed as

X_{t}^{Q_{2}}(y,z)=\sum_{k,\ell\geq 1}x_{k,\ell}^{Q_{2}}(t)e_{k,\ell}(y,z),

where the coordinate process

x_{k,\ell}^{Q_{2}}(t)=\langle X_{t}^{Q_{2}},e_{k,\ell}\rangle_{\theta}=\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda_{k,\ell}t}\langle\xi,e_{k,\ell}\rangle_{\theta}+\sigma\int_{0}^{t}\mu_{k,\ell}^{-\alpha/2}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda_{k,\ell}(t-s)}\mathrm{d}w_{k,\ell}(s)

is described by the stochastic differential equation

(2.5)

\mathrm{d}x_{k,\ell}^{Q_{2}}(t)=-\lambda_{k,\ell}x_{k,\ell}^{Q_{2}}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\sigma\mu_{k,\ell}^{-\alpha/2}\mathrm{d}w_{k,\ell}(t),\quad x_{k,\ell}^{Q_{2}}(0)=\langle\xi,e_{k,\ell}\rangle_{\theta}

and is also represented as

x_{k,\ell}^{Q_{2}}(t)=2\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}X_{t}^{Q_{2}}(y,z)\sin(\pi ky)\sin(\pi\ell z)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\theta_{1}}{2\theta_{2}}y}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\eta_{1}}{2\theta_{2}}z}\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}z.

Consider the case where the SPDE (
1.1
)
is driven by a cylindrical Brownian motion
\{B_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
defined as

(2.6)

\langle B_{t},f\rangle_{\theta}=\sum_{k,\ell\geq 1}\langle f,e_{k,\ell}\rangle_{\theta}w_{k,\ell}(t),\quad f\in H_{\theta},\ t\geq 0

as in the setting of previous studies on parameter estimation
for SPDEs in one space dimension, in other words,
consider the SPDE represented by

(2.7)

\mathrm{d}X_{t}^{I}(y,z)=-A_{\theta}X_{t}^{I}(y,z)\mathrm{d}t+\sigma\mathrm{d}W_{t}^{I}(y,z),

where
I
is the identity operator and
B_{t}=W_{t}^{I}
.
In this case, the coordinate process

x_{k,\ell}^{I}(t)
of the SPDE (
2.7
) is expressed as

x_{k,\ell}^{I}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda_{k,\ell}t}\langle\xi,e_{k,\ell}\rangle_{\theta}

+\sigma\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda_{k,\ell}(t-s)}\mathrm{d}w_{k,\ell}(s)
.
Since there exists a constant
C_{1}>0
such that

\lambda_{k,\ell}\leq C_{1}(k^{2}+\ell^{2})
for
k,\ell\geq 1
,
it follows that for any
t>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|X_{t}^{I}\|_{\theta}^{2}]=\sum_{k,\ell\geq 1}\mathbb{E}[x_{k,\ell}^{I}(t)^{2}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,\ell\geq 1}\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda_{k,\ell}t}\mathbb{E}[\langle\xi,e_{k,\ell}\rangle_{\theta}^{2}]+\sigma^{2}\sum_{k,\ell\geq 1}\frac{1-\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda_{k,\ell}t}}{2\lambda_{k,\ell}}

\displaystyle\geq\sigma^{2}\sum_{k,\ell\geq 1}\frac{1-\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda_{k,\ell}t}}{2\lambda_{k,\ell}}

\displaystyle\geq C\sum_{k,\ell\geq 1}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k,\ell}}=\infty,

and then
\mathbb{E}[X_{t}^{I}(y,z)^{2}]=\infty
for some
(y,z)\in D
.
In order to avoid this inconvenience,
we consider a noise
W_{t}^{Q}
with a damping factor such as

\{\lambda_{k,\ell}^{-\alpha/2}\}_{k,\ell\geq 1}
in (
2.1
).
Let
\mathscr{D}(A_{\theta}^{-1/2})\supset H_{\theta}

be the domain of
A_{\theta}^{-1/2}
.
Define the covariance operator
Q_{1}
on
\mathscr{D}(A_{\theta}^{-1/2})
by

Q_{1}e_{k,\ell}=\lambda_{k,\ell}^{-\alpha}\|e_{k,\ell}\|_{\theta,-1/2}^{2}e_{k,\ell},

where
\alpha>0
,
\|u\|_{\theta,-1/2}=\|A_{\theta}^{-1/2}u\|_{\theta}
,
and then
\{e_{k,\ell}/\|e_{k,\ell}\|_{\theta,-1/2}\}_{k,\ell\geq 1}

is the complete orthonormal system on
\mathscr{D}(A_{\theta}^{-1/2})

and the corresponding eigenvalues of
Q_{1}
are

\lambda_{k,\ell}^{-\alpha}\|e_{k,\ell}\|_{\theta,-1/2}^{2}
.
Noting that

\sum_{k,\ell\geq 1}\|A_{\theta}^{-\alpha/2}e_{k,\ell}\|_{\theta,-1/2}^{2}=\sum_{k,\ell\geq 1}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k,\ell}^{1+\alpha}}<\infty

for
\alpha>0
, the
Q_{1}
-Wiener process
\{W_{t}^{Q_{1}}\}_{t\geq 0}

is well-defined in
\mathscr{D}(A_{\theta}^{-1/2})
and
it follows that for
f\in H_{\theta}
,

\langle W^{Q_{1}}_{t},f\rangle_{\theta}=\sum_{k,\ell\geq 1}\lambda_{k,\ell}^{-\alpha/2}\|e_{k,\ell}\|_{\theta,-1/2}\biggl\langle f,\frac{e_{k,\ell}}{\|e_{k,\ell}\|_{\theta,-1/2}}\biggr\rangle_{\theta}w_{k,\ell}(t)=\sum_{k,\ell\geq 1}\lambda_{k,\ell}^{-\alpha/2}\langle f,e_{k,\ell}\rangle_{\theta}w_{k,\ell}(t),

and thus we obtain (
2.1
).
Refer to
Lord et al.
[
16
]
,
Da Prato and Zabczyk
[
21
]

and
Lototsky and Rozovsky
[
18
]

for details on the
Q
-Wiener process.

Unlike the damping factor
\{\lambda_{k,\ell}^{-\alpha/2}\}_{k,\ell\geq 1}

of the
Q_{1}
-Wiener process,
the damping factor
\{\mu_{k,\ell}^{-\alpha/2}\}_{k,\ell\geq 1}

of the
Q_{2}
-Wiener process
does not include the parameter
\theta
of the differential operator
A_{\theta}
.
Moreover, by setting
\kappa=\theta_{1}/\theta_{2}
,
\eta=\eta_{1}/\theta_{2}
and

\zeta=(\frac{\kappa^{2}+\eta^{2}}{4}-\mu_{0},\kappa,\eta,1)
,
it can be regarded as

\mu_{k,\ell}=\pi^{2}(k^{2}+\ell^{2})+\mu_{0}=-\biggl(\frac{\kappa^{2}+\eta^{2}}{4}-\mu_{0}\biggr)+\frac{\kappa^{2}+\eta^{2}}{4\cdot 1}+\pi^{2}(k^{2}+\ell^{2})\cdot 1,

and it holds that

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\zeta}=\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\theta}
,

H_{\zeta}=H_{\theta}
and

A_{\zeta}e_{k,\ell}=\mu_{k,\ell}e_{k,\ell}
, where

-A_{\zeta}=\biggl(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial y^{2}}+\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z^{2}}\biggr)+\kappa\frac{\partial}{\partial y}+\eta\frac{\partial}{\partial z}+\biggl(\frac{\kappa^{2}+\eta^{2}}{4}-\mu_{0}\biggr).

Hence, the
Q_{2}
-Wiener process can be constructed in the same way.
Indeed, by choosing
Q_{2}
as the covariance operator
on
\mathscr{D}(A_{\zeta}^{-1/2})\supset H_{\zeta}(=H_{\theta})
that satisfies

Q_{2}e_{k,\ell}=\mu_{k,\ell}^{-\alpha}\|e_{k,\ell}\|_{\zeta,-1/2}^{2}e_{k,\ell},

where
\alpha>0
, we can see that
the
Q_{2}
-Wiener process
\{W_{t}^{Q_{2}}\}_{t\geq 0}

is well-defined in
\mathscr{D}(A_{\zeta}^{-1/2})
and

\langle W^{Q_{2}}_{t},f\rangle_{\theta}=\langle W^{Q_{2}}_{t},f\rangle_{\zeta}=\sum_{k,\ell\geq 1}\mu_{k,\ell}^{-\alpha/2}\langle f,e_{k,\ell}\rangle_{\zeta}w_{k,\ell}(t)=\sum_{k,\ell\geq 1}\mu_{k,\ell}^{-\alpha/2}\langle f,e_{k,\ell}\rangle_{\theta}w_{k,\ell}(t)

for
f\in H_{\theta}
and
t\geq 0
.

In this paper, we consider only two types of
Q
-Wiener processes
given by (
2.1
) or (
2.2
).
However, the same argument can be developed by choosing
a
Q
-Wiener process with a damping factor
such that the random field
X_{t}^{Q}(y,z)
is spectrally decomposable
as in (
2.3
).

We assume the following conditions
on the initial value
\xi\in H_{\theta}
of the SPDE (
1.1
).

The initial value
\xi
satisfies either (i) or (ii), and both (iii) and (iv).

(i)

\mathbb{E}[\langle\xi,e_{k,\ell}\rangle_{\theta}]=0
for all
k,\ell\geq 1

and
\sup_{k,\ell\geq 1}\lambda_{k,\ell}^{1+\alpha}\mathbb{E}[\langle\xi,e_{k}\rangle_{\theta}^{2}]<\infty
.

(ii)

\mathbb{E}[\|A_{\theta}^{(1+\alpha)/2}\xi\|_{\theta}^{2}]<\infty
.

(iii)

\sup_{k,\ell\geq 1}\lambda_{k,\ell}^{1+\alpha}\mathbb{E}[\langle\xi,e_{k,\ell}\rangle_{\theta}^{4}]<\infty
.

(iv)

\{\langle\xi,e_{k,\ell}\rangle_{\theta}\}_{k,\ell\geq 1}
are independent.

From
\|A_{\theta}^{(1+\alpha)/2}\xi\|_{\theta}^{2}=\sum_{k,\ell\geq 1}\lambda_{k,\ell}^{1+\alpha}\langle\xi,e_{k,\ell}\rangle_{\theta}^{2}
,
(ii) in Assumption
1
can be replaced by

\sum_{k,\ell\geq 1}\lambda_{k,\ell}^{1+\alpha}\mathbb{E}[\langle\xi,e_{k,\ell}\rangle_{\theta}^{2}]<\infty.

Moreover, for a non-random function
\xi(y,z)=\xi_{1}(y)\xi_{2}(z)\in H_{\theta}
,
if
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in C^{2}([0,1])
for
0<\alpha<1/2
,
or if
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in C^{3}([0,1])
for
1/2\leq\alpha<1
,
then
\xi
satisfies (ii) and (iii).
Indeed, noting that

\langle\xi,e_{k,\ell}\rangle_{\theta}=2\int_{0}^{1}\xi_{1}(y)\sin(\pi ky)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\theta_{1}}{2\theta_{2}}y}\mathrm{d}y\int_{0}^{1}\xi_{2}(z)\sin(\pi\ell z)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\eta_{1}}{2\theta_{2}}z}\mathrm{d}z

and that for
\xi_{1}\in C^{p}([0,1])
(
p=2,3
) such that
\xi_{1}(0)=\xi_{1}(1)=0
,

\Biggl|\int_{0}^{1}\xi_{1}(y)\sin(\pi ky)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\theta_{1}}{2\theta_{2}}y}\mathrm{d}y\Biggr|\leq\frac{C_{1}}{k^{p}},

we obtain that
|\langle\xi,e_{k,\ell}\rangle_{\theta}|\leq C_{2}/k^{p}\ell^{p}
.
Since

\lambda_{k,\ell}\leq C_{3}(k^{2}+\ell^{2})
and

\sum_{k,\ell\geq 1}\lambda_{k,\ell}^{1+\alpha}\langle\xi,e_{k,\ell}\rangle_{\theta}^{2}\leq C\sum_{k,\ell\geq 1}\frac{(k^{2}+\ell^{2})^{1+\alpha}}{k^{2p}\ell^{2p}}\leq C^{\prime}\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{1}{k^{2(p-1)-2\alpha}}\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\frac{1}{\ell^{2p}},

\sum_{k,\ell\geq 1}\lambda_{k,\ell}^{1+\alpha}\langle\xi,e_{k,\ell}\rangle_{\theta}^{2}

converges if
p>\alpha+3/2
, and therefore (ii) is satisfied
by setting
p=2
for
0<\alpha<1/2
and
p=3
for
1/2\leq\alpha<1
.
Similarly,
\xi
also satisfies (iii).

By setting the
Q_{1}
-Wiener process
W_{t}^{Q_{1}}
by (
2.1
),
there exists a unique mild solution
X_{t}^{Q_{1}}
of the SPDE (
1.1
)
which satisfies
\sup_{0\leq t\leq 1}\mathbb{E}[\|X_{t}^{Q_{1}}\|_{\theta}^{2}]<\infty

under
\lambda_{1,1}^{*}>0
and Assumption
1
.
Indeed, since

\sup_{k,\ell\geq 1}\lambda_{k,\ell}^{1+\alpha}\mathbb{E}[\langle\xi,e_{k}\rangle_{\theta}^{2}]<\infty
under (i) or (ii) in Assumption
1

and that there exists a constant
C_{1}>0
such that

\lambda_{k,\ell}\geq C_{1}(k^{2}+\ell^{2})
for
k,\ell\geq 1
,
it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|X_{t}^{Q_{1}}\|_{\theta}^{2}]=\sum_{k,\ell\geq 1}\mathbb{E}[x_{k,\ell}^{Q_{1}}(t)^{2}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,\ell\geq 1}\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda_{k,\ell}t}\mathbb{E}[\langle\xi,e_{k,\ell}\rangle_{\theta}^{2}]+\sigma^{2}\sum_{k,\ell\geq 1}\frac{1-\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda_{k,\ell}t}}{2\lambda_{k,\ell}^{1+\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k,\ell\geq 1}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k,\ell}^{1+\alpha}}\lambda_{k,\ell}^{1+\alpha}\mathbb{E}[\langle\xi,e_{k,\ell}\rangle_{\theta}^{2}]+\sigma^{2}\sum_{k,\ell\geq 1}\frac{1}{2\lambda_{k,\ell}^{1+\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{k,\ell\geq 1}\frac{1}{\lambda_{k,\ell}^{1+\alpha}}<\infty

for
\alpha>0
, and the mild solution
X_{t}^{Q_{1}}
satisfies

\sup_{0\leq t\leq 1}\mathbb{E}[\|X_{t}^{Q_{1}}\|_{\theta}^{2}]<\infty
.
The same is true for
X_{t}^{Q_{2}}
.

3.
Main results

3.1.
SPDE driven by
Q_{1}
-Wiener process

In this subsection, we deal with the SPDE (
1.1
)
driven by the
Q_{1}
-Wiener process defined as (
2.1
).
We first consider the estimation for the parameters
which appear in the coordinate process (
2.4
)
based on the thinned data with respect to space.
Set
\overline{m}_{1}\leq M_{1}
and
\overline{m}_{2}\leq M_{2}

such that
\overline{m}:=\overline{m}_{1}\overline{m}_{2}=O(N^{\rho})

for some
0<\rho<1\land 2(1-\alpha)
, and let

\displaystyle\overline{y}_{j_{1}}

\displaystyle=\biggl\lfloor\frac{M_{1}}{\overline{m}_{1}}\biggr\rfloor\frac{j_{1}}{M_{1}},\quad j_{1}=0,\ldots,\overline{m}_{1},

\displaystyle\overline{z}_{j_{2}}

\displaystyle=\biggl\lfloor\frac{M_{2}}{\overline{m}_{2}}\biggr\rfloor\frac{j_{2}}{M_{2}},\quad j_{2}=0,\ldots,\overline{m}_{2}.

For
\delta\in(0,1/2)
,
there exist
J_{1},J_{2}\geq 1
,
m_{1},m_{2}\geq 1
such that

\displaystyle\overline{y}_{J_{1}}<\delta\leq\overline{y}_{J_{1}+1}<\cdots<\overline{y}_{J_{1}+m_{1}}\leq 1-\delta<\overline{y}_{J_{1}+m_{1}+1},

\displaystyle\overline{z}_{J_{2}}<\delta\leq\overline{z}_{J_{2}+1}<\cdots<\overline{z}_{J_{2}+m_{2}}\leq 1-\delta<\overline{z}_{J_{2}+m_{2}+1},

and let

\displaystyle\widetilde{y}_{j_{1}}

\displaystyle=\overline{y}_{J_{1}+j_{1}}=\biggl\lfloor\frac{M_{1}}{\overline{m}_{1}}\biggr\rfloor\frac{J_{1}+j_{1}}{M_{1}},\quad j_{1}=1,\ldots,m_{1},

\displaystyle\widetilde{z}_{j_{2}}

\displaystyle=\overline{z}_{J_{2}+j_{2}}=\biggl\lfloor\frac{M_{2}}{\overline{m}_{2}}\biggr\rfloor\frac{J_{2}+j_{2}}{M_{2}},\quad j_{2}=1,\ldots,m_{2}

and
D_{\delta}=[\delta,1-\delta]^{2}\subset D
.
Note that
m:=m_{1}m_{2}=O(N^{\rho})
and

(\widetilde{y}_{j_{1}},\widetilde{z}_{j_{2}})\in D_{\delta}

for any
j_{1}=1,\ldots,m_{1}
and
j_{2}=1,\ldots,m_{2}
.

We write
\Delta_{i}X^{Q}(y,z)=X_{t_{i}}^{Q}(y,z)-X_{t_{i-1}}^{Q}(y,z)
.
Let
\Gamma(s)=\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{s-1}\mathrm{e}^{-x}\mathrm{d}x
,
s>0
.

Under Assumption
1
,
it holds that uniformly in
(y,z)\in D_{\delta}
,

(3.1)

\mathbb{E}[(\Delta_{i}X^{Q_{1}})^{2}(y,z)]=\Delta_{N}^{\alpha}\frac{\sigma^{2}\Gamma(1-\alpha)}{4\pi\alpha\theta_{2}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}}y}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\eta_{1}}{\theta_{2}}z}+r_{N,i}+O(\Delta_{N}),

where
\sum_{i=1}^{N}|r_{N,i}|=O(\Delta_{N}^{\alpha})
,
and thus

\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{N\Delta_{N}^{\alpha}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\Delta_{i}X^{Q_{1}})^{2}(y,z)\biggr]=\frac{\sigma^{2}\Gamma(1-\alpha)}{4\pi\alpha\theta_{2}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}}y}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\eta_{1}}{\theta_{2}}z}+O(\Delta_{N}^{1-\alpha}).

It follows from the proofs of Lemmas
5.3
,
5.4

and Proposition
3.1
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\Delta_{i}X^{Q_{1}})^{2}(y,z)]

(3.2)

\displaystyle=\sigma^{2}\sum_{k,\ell\geq 1}\frac{1-\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda_{k,\ell}\Delta_{N}}}{\lambda_{k,\ell}^{1+\alpha}}\biggl(1-\frac{1-\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda_{k,\ell}\Delta_{N}}}{2}\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda_{k,\ell}(i-1)\Delta_{N}}\biggr)e_{k,\ell}^{2}(y,z)+r_{N,i},

where
r_{N,i}
is the sequence in (
3.1
).
According to Lemmas
5.2
and
5.4
,
the restriction of
\alpha
allows us to
approximate the summation in (
3.2
) containing
\lambda_{k,\ell}

with unknown parameters by an explicit expression
such as the main part in (
3.1
).
This makes it possible to estimate

\sigma^{2}/\theta_{2}
,
\theta_{1}/\theta_{2}
and
\eta_{1}/\theta_{2}
.

Let
s=\sigma^{2}/\theta_{2}
,
\kappa=\theta_{1}/\theta_{2}
,
\eta=\eta_{1}/\theta_{2}
and

Z_{N}^{Q}(y,z)=\frac{1}{N\Delta_{N}^{\alpha}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\Delta_{i}X^{Q})^{2}(y,z).

By setting
the contrast function

U_{N,m}^{(1)}(s,\kappa,\eta)=\sum_{j_{1}=1}^{m_{1}}\sum_{j_{2}=1}^{m_{2}}\biggl(Z_{N}^{Q_{1}}(\widetilde{y}_{j_{1}},\widetilde{z}_{j_{2}})-\frac{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}{4\pi\alpha}s\,\mathrm{e}^{-(\kappa\widetilde{y}_{j_{1}}+\eta\widetilde{z}_{j_{2}})}\biggr)^{2},

the minimum contrast estimators of
s
,
\kappa
and
\eta
are defined as

(\hat{s},\hat{\kappa},\hat{\eta})=\underset{(s,\kappa,\eta)\in\Xi_{1}}{\mathrm{arginf}}\,U_{N,m}^{(1)}(s,\kappa,\eta),

where
\Xi_{1}
is a compact convex subset of

(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
We suppose that the true value
(s^{*},\kappa^{*},\eta^{*})

belongs to
\mathrm{Int}\,\Xi_{1}
.

Under Assumption
1
,
it holds that for any
\gamma<\frac{1}{2}\land(1-\alpha)-\frac{\rho}{2}
,
as
N\to\infty
and
m\to\infty
,

m^{1/2}N^{\gamma}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{s}-s^{*}\\
\hat{\kappa}-\kappa^{*}\\
\hat{\eta}-\eta^{*}\end{pmatrix}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}0.

Bibinger and Trabs
[
1
]
,
which dealt with the parameter estimation for linear parabolic SPDEs
in one space dimension, showed that
the estimators have asymptotic normality.
On the other hand, in two space dimensions,
the evaluation of the remainder in (
3.1
)
is worse than that in one space dimension
because of the increase in dimension,
and the asymptotic normality can not be derived.
However, it is possible to estimate each coefficient parameter
even if the estimators do not have asymptotic normality.
Theorem
3.2
shows that in two space dimensions,
the estimators have
m^{1/2}N^{\gamma}
-consistency
and
m^{1/2}N^{\gamma}=o(N^{\frac{1}{2}\land(1-\alpha)})
.
In other words, the assertion in Theorem
3.2
can be regarded as

N^{\gamma^{\prime}}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{s}-s^{*}\\
\hat{\kappa}-\kappa^{*}\\
\hat{\eta}-\eta^{*}\end{pmatrix}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}0

for any
\gamma^{\prime}<\frac{1}{2}\land(1-\alpha)
.

Once we estimate
(s,\kappa,\eta)
, we can construct an approximation for
the coordinate process

x_{k,\ell}^{Q_{1}}(t)=\langle X_{t}^{Q_{1}},e_{k,\ell}\rangle_{\theta}=2\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}X_{t}^{Q_{1}}(y,z)\sin(\pi ky)\sin(\pi\ell z)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\kappa}{2}y}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\eta}{2}z}\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}z

by a Riemann sum. Furthermore,
noting that the coordinate process
x_{k,\ell}^{Q_{1}}(t)
satisfies

\mathrm{d}x_{k,\ell}^{Q_{1}}(t)=-\lambda_{k,\ell}x_{k,\ell}^{Q_{1}}(t)\mathrm{d}t+\sigma\lambda_{k,\ell}^{-\alpha/2}\mathrm{d}w_{k,\ell}(t),\quad x_{k,\ell}^{Q_{1}}(0)=\langle\xi,e_{k,\ell}\rangle_{\theta},

we can estimate the volatility parameter

\sigma_{k,\ell}:=\sigma\lambda_{k,\ell}^{-\alpha/2}

by using statistical inference for diffusion processes. Since

\lambda_{1,2}=\biggl(\frac{\sigma_{1,1}^{2}}{\sigma_{1,2}^{2}}\biggr)^{1/\alpha}\lambda_{1,1},\quad\lambda_{1,1}=\biggl(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\sigma_{1,1}^{2}}\biggr)^{1/\alpha}=\biggl(\frac{s\theta_{2}}{\sigma_{1,1}^{2}}\biggr)^{1/\alpha}

and
\lambda_{1,2}-\lambda_{1,1}=3\pi^{2}\theta_{2}
,

\theta_{2}
can be expressed by using
s
,
\sigma_{1,1}
and
\sigma_{1,2}
as follows.

\theta_{2}=\Biggl\{\frac{3\pi^{2}}{s^{1/\alpha}}\Biggl(\frac{1}{\sigma_{1,2}^{2/\alpha}}-\frac{1}{\sigma_{1,1}^{2/\alpha}}\Biggr)^{-1}\Biggr\}^{\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}}.

\sigma^{2}
,
\theta_{1}
,
\eta_{1}
and
\theta_{0}

can also be expressed by using

\theta_{2}
,
s
,
\kappa
,
\eta
and
\lambda_{1,1}
as follows.

\sigma^{2}=s\theta_{2},\quad\theta_{1}=\kappa\theta_{2},\quad\eta_{1}=\eta\theta_{2},

\theta_{0}=-\lambda_{1,1}+\biggl(\frac{\kappa^{2}+\eta^{2}}{4}+2\pi^{2}\biggr)\theta_{2}.

With the above in mind,
we construct an approximate coordinate process
by using the thinned data with respect to time,
and consider the estimation for each coefficient parameter.
Let
n\leq N
and

\widetilde{t}_{i}=\biggl\lfloor\frac{N}{n}\biggr\rfloor\frac{i}{N},\quad i=0,\ldots,n.

As an approximation of
x_{k,\ell}^{Q_{1}}(t)
, we consider

\hat{x}_{k,\ell}^{Q_{1}}(\widetilde{t}_{i})=\frac{2}{M}\sum_{j_{1}=1}^{M_{1}}\sum_{j_{2}=1}^{M_{2}}X_{\widetilde{t}_{i}}^{Q_{1}}(y_{j_{1}},z_{j_{2}})\sin(\pi ky_{j_{1}})\sin(\pi\ell z_{j_{2}})\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\hat{\kappa}}{2}y_{j_{1}}}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\hat{\eta}}{2}z_{j_{2}}},\quad i=1,\ldots,n.

By using the thinned data based on the approximate coordinate process

\{\hat{x}_{k,\ell}^{Q_{1}}(\widetilde{t}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
,
the estimator of
\sigma_{k,\ell}^{2}
is defined as

\hat{\sigma}_{k,\ell}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(\hat{x}_{k,\ell}^{Q_{1}}(\widetilde{t}_{i})-\hat{x}_{k,\ell}^{Q_{1}}(\widetilde{t}_{i-1})\bigr)^{2}.

Moreover, the estimators of
\theta_{2}
,
\sigma^{2}
,
\theta_{1}
,
\eta_{1}
and
\theta_{0}

are defined as

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{2}

\displaystyle=\Biggl\{\frac{3\pi^{2}}{\hat{s}^{1/\alpha}}\Biggl(\frac{1}{(\hat{\sigma}_{1,2}^{2})^{1/\alpha}}-\frac{1}{(\hat{\sigma}_{1,1}^{2})^{1/\alpha}}\Biggr)^{-1}\Biggr\}^{\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}},

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}^{2}

\displaystyle=\hat{s}\hat{\theta}_{2},\quad\hat{\theta}_{1}=\hat{\kappa}\hat{\theta}_{2},\quad\hat{\eta}_{1}=\hat{\eta}\hat{\theta}_{2},

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{0}

\displaystyle=-\hat{\lambda}_{1,1}+\biggl(\frac{\hat{\kappa}^{2}+\hat{\eta}^{2}}{4}+2\pi^{2}\biggr)\hat{\theta}_{2},\quad\hat{\lambda}_{1,1}=\biggl(\frac{\hat{s}\hat{\theta}_{2}}{\hat{\sigma}_{1,1}^{2}}\biggr)^{1/\alpha}.

Let

\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{-1}^{*}=(\theta_{1}^{*},\eta_{1}^{*},\theta_{2}^{*},(\sigma^{*})^{2})^{\mathsf{T}}
,

\displaystyle c_{1}

\displaystyle=(\lambda_{1,1}^{*})^{2}\biggl(\frac{\lambda_{1,2}^{*}}{\alpha}-\frac{\theta_{0}^{*}}{1-\alpha}\biggr)^{2}+(\lambda_{1,2}^{*})^{2}\biggl(\frac{\lambda_{1,1}^{*}}{\alpha}-\frac{\theta_{0}^{*}}{1-\alpha}\biggr)^{2},

\displaystyle c_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{1-\alpha}\biggl\{(\lambda_{1,1}^{*})^{2}\biggl(\frac{\lambda_{1,2}^{*}}{\alpha}-\frac{\theta_{0}^{*}}{1-\alpha}\biggr)+(\lambda_{1,2}^{*})^{2}\biggl(\frac{\lambda_{1,1}^{*}}{\alpha}-\frac{\theta_{0}^{*}}{1-\alpha}\biggr)\biggr\},

\displaystyle c_{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(1-\alpha)^{2}}\bigl\{(\lambda_{1,1}^{*})^{2}+(\lambda_{1,2}^{*})^{2}\bigr\}

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{9\pi^{4}(\theta_{2}^{*})^{2}}\begin{pmatrix}c_{1}&c_{2}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{-1}^{*})^{\mathsf{T}}\\
c_{2}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{-1}^{*}&c_{3}\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{-1}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\vartheta}_{-1}^{*})^{\mathsf{T}}\end{pmatrix},

where
\mathsf{T}
denotes the transpose.

Suppose Assumption
1
holds.
Let
\gamma<\frac{1}{2}\land(1-\alpha)-\frac{\rho}{2}
.

(1)

Under
\frac{n^{1-\alpha}}{mN^{2\gamma}}\to 0
and

\frac{n^{1-\alpha+\epsilon}}{M_{1}^{2\epsilon}\land M_{2}^{2\epsilon}}\to 0

for some
\epsilon<\alpha
, it holds that as
n\to\infty
,

(\hat{\theta}_{0},\hat{\theta}_{1},\hat{\eta}_{1},\hat{\theta}_{2},\hat{\sigma}^{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\theta_{0}^{*},\theta_{1}^{*},\eta_{1}^{*},\theta_{2}^{*},(\sigma^{*})^{2}).

(2)

Under
\frac{n^{2-\alpha}}{mN^{2\gamma}}\to 0
and

\frac{n^{2-\alpha+\epsilon}}{M_{1}^{2\epsilon}\land M_{2}^{2\epsilon}}\to 0

for some
\epsilon<\alpha
, it holds that as
n\to\infty
,

\sqrt{n}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\theta}_{0}-\theta_{0}^{*}\\
\hat{\theta}_{1}-\theta_{1}^{*}\\
\hat{\eta}_{1}-\eta_{1}^{*}\\
\hat{\theta}_{2}-\theta_{2}^{*}\\
\hat{\sigma}^{2}-(\sigma^{*})^{2}\end{pmatrix}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}N(0,J).

Set
M_{1}=M_{2}
,
M=O(N^{\beta})
,
\beta>0
,

n=O(N^{\zeta})
,
0<\zeta\leq 1
,
and
m=O(N^{\rho})
,
0<\rho<1\land 2(1-\alpha)
.
For the condition of (1) in Theorem
3.3
, one has from

\frac{n^{1-\alpha}}{mN^{2\gamma}}=O(N^{\zeta(1-\alpha)-\rho-2\gamma})
,

\frac{n^{1-\alpha+\epsilon}}{M_{1}^{2\epsilon}\land M_{2}^{2\epsilon}}=O(N^{\zeta(1-\alpha+\epsilon)-\epsilon\beta})

that

(3.3)

\zeta(1-\alpha)-\rho-2\gamma<0,\quad\zeta(1-\alpha+\epsilon)-\epsilon\beta<0

for some
\epsilon<\alpha
.
For the existence of
\epsilon<\alpha
such that

\zeta(1-\alpha+\epsilon)-\epsilon\beta<0
,
it suffices that

\zeta(1-\alpha)/(\beta-\zeta)<\epsilon<\alpha
,
i.e.,
\zeta<\alpha\beta
.
Therefore, (
3.3
) is fulfilled if
\zeta
is chosen as follows.

(3.4)

\zeta<\frac{\rho+2\gamma}{1-\alpha}\land\alpha\beta.

Since we can choose
\gamma
such that
1-\alpha<\rho+2\gamma
,
(
3.4
) is represented by
\zeta<\alpha\beta
.
In the same way as (2) in Theorem
3.3
,
we can choose
0<\zeta\leq 1
such that

(3.5)

\zeta<\frac{\rho+2\gamma}{2-\alpha}\land\frac{\alpha\beta}{2},

and since
\gamma
can be chosen to satisfy
\rho+2\gamma<1\land 2(1-\alpha)
,
(
3.5
) is represented by

\zeta<\frac{1\land 2(1-\alpha)}{2-\alpha}\land\frac{\alpha\beta}{2}
.
We also see from (
3.4
) and (
3.5
) that
for some
\zeta<\frac{\rho+2\gamma}{2-\alpha}
,
we need to set
\beta>\zeta/\alpha
for consistency,
and
\beta>2\zeta/\alpha
for asymptotic normality.
That is, in order to obtain asymptotic normality of the estimators,
it is necessary to increase the number of observations in space
M
compared to
the number of observations in space required for consistency to hold.

3.2.
SPDE driven by
Q_{2}
-Wiener process

In this subsection, we consider the SPDE (
1.1
)
driven by the
Q_{2}
-Wiener process defined as (
2.2
).
In this case, as in Subsection
3.1
,
we first estimate the parameters
\theta_{1}/\theta_{2}
and
\eta_{1}/\theta_{2}

which appear in the coordinate process,
and then estimate the coefficient parameters
using the approximate coordinate process.

In a similar way to Proposition
3.1
, the following proposition holds.

Under Assumption
1
,
it holds that uniformly in
(y,z)\in D_{\delta}
,

(3.6)

\mathbb{E}[(\Delta_{i}X^{Q_{2}})^{2}(y,z)]=\Delta_{N}^{\alpha}\frac{\sigma^{2}\Gamma(1-\alpha)}{4\pi\alpha\theta_{2}^{1-\alpha}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}}y}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\eta_{1}}{\theta_{2}}z}+r_{N,i}+O(\Delta_{N}),

where
\sum_{i=1}^{N}|r_{N,i}|=O(\Delta_{N}^{\alpha})
,
and thus

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{N\Delta_{N}^{\alpha}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\Delta_{i}X^{Q_{2}})^{2}(y,z)\biggr]

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma^{2}\Gamma(1-\alpha)}{4\pi\alpha\theta_{2}^{1-\alpha}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\theta_{1}}{\theta_{2}}y}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\eta_{1}}{\theta_{2}}z}+O(\Delta_{N}^{1-\alpha}).

The only difference between (
3.1
) and (
3.6
) is
the exponent of
\theta_{2}
in the denominator.
This is caused by the fact that the coefficients of
k^{2}+\ell^{2}

in
\lambda_{k,\ell}
and
\mu_{k,\ell}
are different,
which are
\theta_{2}\pi^{2}
and
\pi^{2}
respectively.
See Lemmas
5.1
and
5.5
for details.
Proposition
3.4
allows us to estimate

\sigma^{2}/\theta_{2}^{1-\alpha}
,
\theta_{1}/\theta_{2}
and
\eta_{1}/\theta_{2}

when the SPDE (
1.1
) is driven by the
Q_{2}
-Wiener process.

Let
S=\sigma^{2}/\theta_{2}^{1-\alpha}
,
\kappa=\theta_{1}/\theta_{2}
,
and
\eta=\eta_{1}/\theta_{2}
, and let

U_{N,m}^{(2)}(S,\kappa,\eta)=\sum_{j_{1}=1}^{m_{1}}\sum_{j_{2}=1}^{m_{2}}\biggl(Z_{N}^{Q_{2}}(\widetilde{y}_{j_{1}},\widetilde{z}_{j_{2}})-\frac{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}{4\pi\alpha}S\,\mathrm{e}^{-(\kappa\widetilde{y}_{j_{1}}+\eta\widetilde{z}_{j_{2}})}\biggr)^{2},

which is the contrast function of
S
,
\kappa
and
\eta
.
Let
\check{S}
,
\check{\kappa}
and
\check{\eta}
be
minimum contrast estimators defined as

(\check{S},\check{\kappa},\check{\eta})=\underset{(S,\kappa,\eta)\in\Xi_{2}}{\mathrm{arginf}}\,U_{N,m}^{(2)}(S,\kappa,\eta),

where
\Xi_{2}
is a compact convex subset of

(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and we assume that
the true value
(S^{*},\kappa^{*},\eta^{*})
belongs to
\mathrm{Int}\,\Xi_{2}
.

Under Assumption
1
,
it holds that for any
\gamma<\frac{1}{2}\land(1-\alpha)-\frac{\rho}{2}
,
as
N\to\infty
and
m\to\infty
,

m^{1/2}N^{\gamma}\begin{pmatrix}\check{S}-S^{*}\\
\check{\kappa}-\kappa^{*}\\
\check{\eta}-\eta^{*}\end{pmatrix}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}0.

We construct the following approximate coordinate process
by using the estimators
\check{\kappa}
and
\check{\eta}
.

\check{x}_{k,\ell}^{Q_{2}}(\widetilde{t}_{i})=\frac{2}{M}\sum_{j_{1}=1}^{M_{1}}\sum_{j_{2}=1}^{M_{2}}X_{\widetilde{t}_{i}}^{Q_{2}}(y_{j_{1}},z_{j_{2}})\sin(\pi ky_{j_{1}})\sin(\pi\ell z_{j_{2}})\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\hat{\kappa}}{2}y_{j_{1}}}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\hat{\eta}}{2}z_{j_{2}}},\quad i=1,\ldots,n.

Noting that the coordinate process
x_{k,\ell}^{Q_{2}}(t)
is
a diffusion process given by (
2.5
),
we can estimate the volatility parameter

\tau_{k,\ell}:=\sigma\mu_{k,\ell}^{-\alpha/2}
.

If
\mu_{0}
is known, then the estimator of
\sigma^{2}
is defined as

\check{\sigma}^{2}=\mu_{1,1}^{\alpha}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(\check{x}_{1,1}^{Q_{2}}(\widetilde{t}_{i})-\check{x}_{1,1}^{Q_{2}}(\widetilde{t}_{i-1})\bigr)^{2},

and the estimators of
\theta_{2}
,
\theta_{1}
and
\eta_{1}
are defined as

\check{\theta}_{2}=\biggl(\frac{\check{\sigma}^{2}}{\check{S}}\biggr)^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}},\quad\check{\theta}_{1}=\check{\kappa}\check{\theta}_{2},\quad\check{\eta}_{1}=\check{\eta}\check{\theta}_{2}.

On the other hand, since

\mu_{1,1}=\biggl(\frac{\tau_{1,2}^{2}}{\tau_{1,1}^{2}}\biggr)^{1/\alpha}\mu_{1,2},\quad\mu_{1,2}=\mu_{1,1}+3\pi^{2},

\mu_{1,1}
and
\sigma^{2}
can be expressed as

\mu_{1,1}=\frac{3\pi^{2}(\tau_{1,2}^{2}/\tau_{1,1}^{2})^{1/\alpha}}{1-(\tau_{1,2}^{2}/\tau_{1,1}^{2})^{1/\alpha}}=\frac{3\pi^{2}}{\tau_{1,1}^{2/\alpha}}\biggl(\frac{1}{\tau_{1,2}^{2/\alpha}}-\frac{1}{\tau_{1,1}^{2/\alpha}}\biggr)^{-1},

\sigma^{2}=\tau_{1,1}^{2}\mu_{1,1}^{\alpha}=\Biggl\{3\pi^{2}\biggl(\frac{1}{\tau_{1,2}^{2/\alpha}}-\frac{1}{\tau_{1,1}^{2/\alpha}}\biggr)^{-1}\Biggr\}^{\alpha}.

Therefore, if
\mu_{0}
is unknown, then
the estimators of
\tau_{k,\ell}^{2}
and
\sigma^{2}
are defined as

\bar{\tau}_{k,\ell}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(\check{x}_{k,\ell}^{Q_{2}}(\widetilde{t}_{i})-\check{x}_{k,\ell}^{Q_{2}}(\widetilde{t}_{i-1})\bigr)^{2},

\bar{\sigma}^{2}=\Biggl\{3\pi^{2}\biggl(\frac{1}{(\bar{\tau}_{1,2}^{2})^{1/\alpha}}-\frac{1}{(\bar{\tau}_{1,1}^{2})^{1/\alpha}}\biggr)^{-1}\Biggr\}^{\alpha}

and the estimators of
\theta_{2}
,
\theta_{1}
,
\eta_{1}
and
\mu_{0}
are defined as

\bar{\theta}_{2}=\biggl(\frac{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}{\check{S}}\biggr)^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}},\quad\bar{\theta}_{1}=\check{\kappa}\bar{\theta}_{2},\quad\bar{\eta}_{1}=\check{\eta}\bar{\theta}_{2},

\bar{\mu}_{0}=\bar{\mu}_{1,1}-2\pi^{2}=\frac{3\pi^{2}}{(\bar{\tau}_{1,1}^{2})^{1/\alpha}}\biggl(\frac{1}{(\bar{\tau}_{1,2}^{2})^{1/\alpha}}-\frac{1}{(\bar{\tau}_{1,1}^{2})^{1/\alpha}}\biggr)^{-1}-2\pi^{2}.

Let
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{-1}^{*}=(\theta_{1}^{*},\eta_{1}^{*},\theta_{2}^{*},(1-\alpha)(\sigma^{*})^{2})^{\mathsf{T}}
,

d_{1}=\frac{2(\mu_{1,1}^{*})^{2}(\mu_{1,2}^{*})^{2}}{\alpha^{2}},\quad d_{2}=\frac{\mu_{1,1}^{*}\mu_{1,2}^{*}(\mu_{1,1}^{*}+\mu_{1,2}^{*})}{\alpha(1-\alpha)},\quad d_{3}=\frac{(\mu_{1,1}^{*})^{2}+(\mu_{1,2}^{*})^{2}}{(1-\alpha)^{2}},

K=\frac{2}{(1-\alpha)^{2}}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{-1}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{-1}^{*})^{\mathsf{T}},\quad L=\frac{2}{9\pi^{4}}\begin{pmatrix}d_{1}&d_{2}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{-1}^{*})^{\mathsf{T}}\\
d_{2}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{-1}^{*}&d_{3}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{-1}^{*}(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{-1}^{*})^{\mathsf{T}}\end{pmatrix}.

Suppose Assumption
1
holds.
Let
\gamma<\frac{1}{2}\land(1-\alpha)-\frac{\rho}{2}
.

(a)

Let
\mu_{0}
be known.

(1)

Under
\frac{n^{1-\alpha}}{mN^{2\gamma}}\to 0
and

\frac{n^{1-\alpha+\epsilon}}{M_{1}^{2\epsilon}\land M_{2}^{2\epsilon}}\to 0

for some
\epsilon<\alpha
,
it holds that as
n\to\infty
,

(\check{\theta}_{1},\check{\eta}_{1},\check{\theta}_{2},\check{\sigma}^{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\theta_{1}^{*},\eta_{1}^{*},\theta_{2}^{*},(\sigma^{*})^{2}).

(2)

Under
\frac{n^{2-\alpha}}{mN^{2\gamma}}\to 0
and

\frac{n^{2-\alpha+\epsilon}}{M_{1}^{2\epsilon}\land M_{2}^{2\epsilon}}\to 0

for some
\epsilon<\alpha
, it holds that as
n\to\infty
,

\sqrt{n}\begin{pmatrix}\check{\theta}_{1}-\theta_{1}^{*}\\
\check{\eta}_{1}-\eta_{1}^{*}\\
\check{\theta}_{2}-\theta_{2}^{*}\\
\check{\sigma}^{2}-(\sigma^{*})^{2}\end{pmatrix}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}N(0,K).

(b)

Let
\mu_{0}
be unknown.

(1)

Under
\frac{n^{1-\alpha}}{mN^{2\gamma}}\to 0
and

\frac{n^{1-\alpha+\epsilon}}{M_{1}^{2\epsilon}\land M_{2}^{2\epsilon}}\to 0

for some
\epsilon<\alpha
,
it holds that as
n\to\infty
,

(\bar{\mu}_{0},\bar{\theta}_{1},\bar{\eta}_{1},\bar{\theta}_{2},\bar{\sigma}^{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\mu_{0}^{*},\theta_{1}^{*},\eta_{1}^{*},\theta_{2}^{*},(\sigma^{*})^{2}).

(2)

Under
\frac{n^{2-\alpha}}{mN^{2\gamma}}\to 0
and

\frac{n^{2-\alpha+\epsilon}}{M_{1}^{2\epsilon}\land M_{2}^{2\epsilon}}\to 0

for some
\epsilon<\alpha
, it holds that as
n\to\infty
,

\sqrt{n}\begin{pmatrix}\bar{\mu}_{0}-\mu_{0}^{*}\\
\bar{\theta}_{1}-\theta_{1}^{*}\\
\bar{\eta}_{1}-\eta_{1}^{*}\\
\bar{\theta}_{2}-\theta_{2}^{*}\\
\bar{\sigma}^{2}-(\sigma^{*})^{2}\end{pmatrix}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}N(0,L).

If
\mu_{0}
is known, then the damping factor
\mu_{k,\ell}^{-\alpha/2}

of the
Q_{2}
-Wiener process is known.
Moreover, since the cylindrical Brownian motion is given by (
2.6
),
the value corresponding to
\mu_{k,\ell}^{-\alpha/2}
in (
2.2
) is
1
,
that is, it is known.
Therefore, it is natural to consider the
Q_{2}
-Wiener process when
\mu_{0}
is known,
since the
Q_{2}
-Wiener process can be regarded as the driving noise
corresponding to a cylindrical Brownian motion
in the sense that the value of the damping factor is known.
Indeed, (a)-(2) in Theorem
3.6
, especially for
\alpha=0.5
,
corresponds to the result of Kaino and Uchida
[
13
]
.
On the other hand,
when
\mu_{0}
is unknown, the
Q_{2}
-Wiener process corresponds to
the
Q_{1}
-Wiener process in the sense that the damping factor is unknown.
Theorem
3.3
and (b) in Theorem
3.6
show that

\theta_{0}
can be estimated
for the SPDE (
1.1
) driven by the
Q_{1}
-Wiener process,
while
\mu_{0}
can be estimated instead of
\theta_{0}

for the SPDE (
1.1
) driven by the
Q_{2}
-Wiener process.

4.
Simulations

The numerical solution of the SPDE (
1.1
) is generated by

(4.1)

\displaystyle\tilde{X}^{Q_{1}}_{t_{i}}(y_{j_{1}},z_{j_{2}})=\sum^{K}_{k=1}\sum^{L}_{\ell=1}x^{Q_{1}}_{k,\ell}(t_{i})e_{k,\ell}(y_{j_{1}},z_{j_{2}}),\quad i=1,...,N,j_{1}=1,...,M_{1},j_{2}=1,...,M_{2}.

For the characteristics of the parameters of the SPDE (
1.1
),
see the Appendix below.
In this simulation, the true values of parameters

(\theta_{0}^{*},\theta_{1}^{*},\eta_{1}^{*},\theta_{2}^{*},\sigma^{*})=(0,0.2,0.2,0.2,1)
.
We set that
N=10^{3}
,
M_{1}=M_{2}=200
,
K=L=10^{5}
,

\xi=0
,

\alpha=0.5
,

\lambda_{1,1}^{*}\approx 4.05
.
When
N=10^{3},M_{1}=M_{2}=200
, the size of data is about 10 GB.
We used R language to compute the estimators of Theorems
3.2
and
3.3
.
The computation time of (
4.1
) is directly proportional to
K\times L
.
Therefore, the computation time for the numerical solution of
the SPDE (
1.1
) is
directly proportional to
N\times M_{1}\times M_{2}\times K\times L
.
In the setting of this simulation,
N\times M_{1}\times M_{2}\times K\times L=4\times 10^{17}
.
Three personal computers were used for this simulation,
and it takes about 100h to generate one sample path of the SPDE (
1.1
).
The number of iteration is
25
because the calculation time is enormous.

-
3
are sample paths of
X_{t}^{Q_{1}}(y,z)
when
(\theta_{0}^{*},\theta_{1}^{*},\eta_{1}^{*},\theta_{2}^{*},\sigma^{*})=(0,0.2,0.2,0.2,1)
.

.

.

and that near

.

and

.

, it can be seen that there is not much difference
between the variation of the sample path near
y=0
and that near
y=1
.

5.
Proofs

We set the following notation.

1.

Let
\mathbb{R}_{+}=(0,\infty)
and
\boldsymbol{k}=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
.

2.

For
A,B\geq 0
, we write
A\lesssim B
if
A\leq CB
for some constant
C>0
.

3.

For
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
we write
\partial_{x_{1}}f(x)=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}f(x)
,

\partial f(x)=(\partial_{x_{1}}f(x),\ldots,\partial_{x_{d}}f(x))

and
\partial^{2}f(x)=(\partial_{x_{j}}\partial_{x_{i}}f(x))_{i,j=1}^{d}
.

4.

For two functions
f
and
g
, we write

f(s)\sim g(s)
(
s\to s_{0}
)
if there exists
c\in(0,\infty)
such that

\lim_{s\to s_{0}}f(s)/g(s)=c
.

5.

Let
\boldsymbol{1}_{A}
be the indicator function of
A
.

6.

Let
\mathrm{i}
be the imaginary unit, and
let
\mathrm{Re}(z)
and
\mathrm{Im}(z)
denote the real and imaginary parts of
z\in\mathbb{C}
,
respectively.

5.1.
Proofs of lemmas

In this subsection,
we prepare some lemmas before proving our assertions in Section
3
.
For
\beta<2
, let
\mathcal{F}_{\beta}
be the space of
all twice continuously differentiable
functions
f:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying the following conditions.

(a)

sf(s^{2})
,

s^{2}f^{\prime}(s^{2})\boldsymbol{1}_{[1,\infty)}
,

s^{3}f^{\prime\prime}(s^{2})\boldsymbol{1}_{[1,\infty)}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
.

(b)

s^{\beta}f(s^{2})\sim 1
(
s\downarrow 0
).

For
f\in\mathcal{F}_{\beta}
, we define

R_{1,N}=O\Biggl(\Delta_{N}\biggl(\int_{\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}^{1}s|f^{\prime}(s^{2})|\mathrm{d}s\lor\int_{\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}^{1}s^{3}|f^{\prime\prime}(s^{2})|\mathrm{d}s\biggr)\Biggr)

R_{2,N}=O\Biggl(\Delta_{N}^{3/2}\int_{\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}^{1}|f^{\prime}(s^{2})|\mathrm{d}s\Biggr),\quad R_{N}^{(\beta)}=O\Biggl(\Delta_{N}^{1/2}\int_{\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}^{1}s^{-\beta}\mathrm{d}s\Biggr).

Let

\displaystyle D_{N}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\biggl\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\biggl|0<x_{1}\leq\frac{\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}{2},\ \frac{\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}{2}<x_{2}\biggl\},

\displaystyle D_{N}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\biggl\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\biggl|\frac{\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}{2}<x_{1},\ 0<x_{2}\leq\frac{\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}{2}\biggl\}.

If
f\in\mathcal{F}_{\beta}
, then the followings hold.

(1)

\displaystyle\Delta_{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}f(\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}\Delta_{N})=\frac{1}{4\pi\theta_{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}f(s)\mathrm{d}s-\int_{D_{N}^{(1)}\cup D_{N}^{(2)}}f(\theta_{2}\pi^{2}|x|^{2})\mathrm{d}x+O(R_{1,N}\lor\Delta_{N}^{1-\beta/2})
.

(2)

For any
y,z\in[\delta,1-\delta]
,

\displaystyle\Delta_{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}f(\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}\Delta_{N})\cos(2\pi k_{1}y)

\displaystyle=-\int_{D_{N}^{(1)}}f(\theta_{2}\pi^{2}|x|^{2})\mathrm{d}x+O\biggl(\frac{R_{1,N}\lor R_{2,N}}{\delta^{2}}\biggr),

\displaystyle\Delta_{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}f(\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}\Delta_{N})\cos(2\pi k_{2}z)

\displaystyle=-\int_{D_{N}^{(2)}}f(\theta_{2}\pi^{2}|x|^{2})\mathrm{d}x+O\biggl(\frac{R_{1,N}\lor R_{2,N}}{\delta^{2}}\biggr).

(3)

For any
y,z\in[\delta,1-\delta]
,

\Delta_{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}f(\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}\Delta_{N})\cos(2\pi k_{1}y)\cos(2\pi k_{2}z)=O\biggl(\frac{R_{1,N}\lor R_{2,N}\lor\Delta_{N}^{1-\beta/2}}{\delta^{3}}\biggr).

Furthermore, it holds that

(5.1)

\int_{D_{N}^{(1)}\cup D_{N}^{(2)}}f(\theta_{2}\pi^{2}|x|^{2})\mathrm{d}x=O(R_{N}^{(\beta)}).

Proof.

Let
K_{N}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}|c_{1}\Delta_{N}^{1/2}<|x|\}
for
c_{1}>0
.
Let
\varphi:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
be a function satisfying (a),
and let
h(x)=\varphi(c_{2}|x|^{2})
,

c_{2}>0
.
Since

\displaystyle\int_{\{x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}|c_{1}\Delta_{N}^{1/2}<|x|\leq 1\}}|\varphi^{\prime}(c_{2}|x|^{2})|\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\mathrm{d}\theta\int_{c_{1}\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}^{1}s|\varphi^{\prime}(c_{2}s^{2})|\mathrm{d}s=O\biggl(\int_{\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}^{1}s|\varphi^{\prime}(s^{2})|\mathrm{d}s\biggr),

\displaystyle\int_{\{x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}|1<|x|\}}|\varphi^{\prime}(c_{2}|x|^{2})|\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\mathrm{d}\theta\int_{1}^{\infty}s|\varphi^{\prime}(c_{2}s^{2})|\mathrm{d}s=O(1),

we have

(5.2)

\displaystyle\int_{K_{N}}|\varphi^{\prime}(c_{2}|x|^{2})|\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=O\biggl(\int_{\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}^{1}s|\varphi^{\prime}(s^{2})|\mathrm{d}s\lor 1\biggr)=O\biggl(\int_{\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}^{1}s|\varphi^{\prime}(s^{2})|\mathrm{d}s\biggr).

Similarly, we obtain

(5.3)

\displaystyle\int_{K_{N}}|x|^{2}|\varphi^{\prime\prime}(c_{2}|x|^{2})|\mathrm{d}x=O\biggl(\int_{\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}^{1}s^{3}|\varphi^{\prime\prime}(s^{2})|\mathrm{d}s\biggr).

Therefore, it follows from (
5.2
), (
5.3
) and

\displaystyle\partial^{2}h(x)

\displaystyle=4c_{2}^{2}\varphi^{\prime\prime}(c_{2}|x|^{2})\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}^{2}&x_{1}x_{2}\\
x_{1}x_{2}&x_{2}^{2}\end{pmatrix}+2c_{2}\varphi^{\prime}(c_{2}|x|^{2})\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}

that

(5.4)

\displaystyle\Delta_{N}\int_{K_{N}}|\partial^{2}h(x)|\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=O\Biggl(\Delta_{N}\biggl(\int_{K_{N}}|x|^{2}|\varphi^{\prime\prime}(c_{2}|x|^{2})|\mathrm{d}x+\int_{K_{N}}|\varphi^{\prime}(c_{2}|x|^{2})|\mathrm{d}x\biggr)\Biggr)=O(R_{1,N}).

(1) Let
g(x)=f(\theta_{2}\pi^{2}|x|^{2})
,

a_{N}^{(k)}=(k+1/2)\Delta_{N}^{1/2}
for
k\geq 0
,

\displaystyle E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}

\displaystyle=(a_{N}^{(k_{1}-1)},a_{N}^{(k_{1})}]\times(a_{N}^{(k_{2}-1)},a_{N}^{(k_{2})}]\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2},

E_{N}=\bigcup_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}

and
F_{N}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}|(2\theta_{2}\pi^{2}\land\lambda_{1,1})^{1/2}\Delta_{N}^{1/2}<|x|\}
.
By the Taylor expansion and (
5.4
), it follows that

\displaystyle\Biggl|\Delta_{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}f(\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}\Delta_{N})-\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\int_{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}f(\theta_{2}\pi^{2}|\boldsymbol{k}|^{2}\Delta_{N})\mathrm{d}x\Biggr|

\displaystyle=\Biggl|\Delta_{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\biggl(f(\theta_{2}\pi^{2}|\boldsymbol{k}|^{2}\Delta_{N})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\int_{0}^{1}f^{\prime}(\theta_{2}\pi^{2}|\boldsymbol{k}|^{2}\Delta_{N}+u(\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}-\theta_{2}\pi^{2}|\boldsymbol{k}|^{2})\Delta_{N})\mathrm{d}u\Bigl(\frac{\theta_{1}^{2}+\eta_{1}^{2}}{4\theta_{2}}-\theta_{0}\Bigr)\Delta_{N}\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\int_{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}f(\theta_{2}\pi^{2}|\boldsymbol{k}|^{2}\Delta_{N})\mathrm{d}x\Biggr|

\displaystyle\leq\Delta_{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\bigl|f^{\prime}(\theta_{2}\pi^{2}|\boldsymbol{k}|^{2}\Delta_{N}+u(\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}-\theta_{2}\pi^{2}|\boldsymbol{k}|^{2})\Delta_{N})\bigr|\mathrm{d}u\Bigl|\frac{\theta_{1}^{2}+\eta_{1}^{2}}{4\theta_{2}}-\theta_{0}\Bigr|\Delta_{N}

(5.5)

\displaystyle=O\biggl(\Delta_{N}\int_{F_{N}}|f^{\prime}(|x|^{2})|\mathrm{d}x\biggr)=O(R_{1,N})

and that

\displaystyle\Biggl|\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\int_{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}f(\theta_{2}\pi^{2}|\boldsymbol{k}|^{2}\Delta_{N})\mathrm{d}x-\int_{E_{N}}f(\theta_{2}\pi^{2}|x|^{2})\mathrm{d}x\Biggr|

\displaystyle=\Biggl|\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\int_{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}\bigl(g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})-g(x)\bigr)\mathrm{d}x\Biggr|

\displaystyle=\Biggl|\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\int_{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}\biggl(\partial g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})(x-\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{2}(x-\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})^{\mathsf{T}}\int_{0}^{1}\partial^{2}g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2}+u(x-\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2}))\mathrm{d}u(x-\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\biggr)\mathrm{d}x\Biggr|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Biggl|\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\int_{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}(x-\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})^{\mathsf{T}}\int_{0}^{1}\partial^{2}g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2}+u(x-\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2}))\mathrm{d}u(x-\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\mathrm{d}x\Biggr|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\int_{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}\int_{0}^{1}\bigl|\partial^{2}g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2}+u(x-\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2}))\bigr|\mathrm{d}u|x-\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2}|^{2}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\lesssim\Delta_{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\int_{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}|\partial^{2}g(x)|\mathrm{d}x

(5.6)

\displaystyle=O\biggl(\Delta_{N}\int_{E_{N}}|\partial^{2}g(x)|\mathrm{d}x\biggr)=O(R_{1,N}).

Therefore, it follows from (
5.5
) and (
5.6
) that

(5.7)

\displaystyle\Delta_{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}f(\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}\Delta_{N})

\displaystyle=\int_{E_{N}}f(\theta_{2}\pi^{2}|x|^{2})\mathrm{d}x+O(R_{1,N}).

Set
D_{N}^{(3)}=\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}|0<x_{1},x_{2}\leq\Delta_{N}^{1/2}/2\}

and
D_{N}=\bigcup_{j=1,2,3}D_{N}^{(j)}
.
Since
sf(s^{2})\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}|f(\theta_{2}\pi^{2}|x|^{2})|\mathrm{d}x<\infty

and

\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}f(\theta_{2}\pi^{2}|x|^{2})\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}\theta_{2}}\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\mathrm{d}\theta\int_{0}^{\infty}sf(s^{2})\mathrm{d}s=\frac{1}{4\pi\theta_{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}f(s)\mathrm{d}s,

(
5.7
) can be expressed from
E_{N}=\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\setminus D_{N}
as

(5.8)

\Delta_{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}f(\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}\Delta_{N})=\frac{1}{4\pi\theta_{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}f(s)\mathrm{d}s-\int_{D_{N}}f(\theta_{2}\pi^{2}|x|^{2})\mathrm{d}x+O(R_{1,N}).

Let
a_{N}=\Delta_{N}^{1/2}/\sqrt{2}
.
From
s^{\beta}f(s^{2})\sim 1

(s\downarrow 0)
,
we see that
s|f(s^{2})|\lesssim s^{1-\beta}
in the vicinity of
s=0

and thus

\displaystyle\int_{D_{N}^{(3)}}|f(\theta_{2}\pi^{2}|x|^{2})|\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{a_{N}}s|f(\theta_{2}\pi^{2}s^{2})|\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=O\biggl(\int_{0}^{\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}s|f(s^{2})|\mathrm{d}s\biggr)

\displaystyle=O\biggl(\int_{0}^{\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}s^{1-\beta}\mathrm{d}s\biggr)=O(\Delta_{N}^{1-\beta/2}).

Therefore, from (
5.8
) and

\displaystyle\int_{D_{N}}f(\theta_{2}\pi^{2}|x|^{2})\mathrm{d}x=\int_{D_{N}^{(1)}\cup D_{N}^{(2)}}f(\theta_{2}\pi^{2}|x|^{2})\mathrm{d}x+O(\Delta_{N}^{1-\beta/2}),

the proof of (1) is complete.
Furthermore, it follows from
\arcsin x\leq\pi x/2
and

|f(s^{2})|\lesssim s^{-\beta}
that

\displaystyle\int_{D_{N}^{(1)}}|f(\theta_{2}\pi^{2}|x|^{2})|\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\int_{a_{N}}^{\infty}s|f(\theta_{2}\pi^{2}s^{2})|\int_{0}^{\arcsin(\Delta_{N}^{1/2}/2s)}\mathrm{d}\theta\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{a_{N}}^{\infty}s|f(\theta_{2}\pi^{2}s^{2})|\arcsin\biggl(\frac{\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}{2s}\biggr)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=O\biggl(\Delta_{N}^{1/2}\int_{\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}^{\infty}|f(s^{2})|\mathrm{d}s\biggr)

\displaystyle=O\biggl(\Delta_{N}^{1/2}\int_{\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}^{1}s^{-\beta}\mathrm{d}s\biggr)=O(R_{N}^{(\beta)}),

and thus we obtain (
5.1
).

(3) In a similar way to (
5.5
), it holds that

\displaystyle\Delta_{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}f(\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}\Delta_{N})\cos(2\pi k_{1}y)\cos(2\pi k_{2}z)

(5.9)

\displaystyle=\Delta_{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\cos(2\pi k_{1}y)\cos(2\pi k_{2}z)+O(R_{1,N}).

For a real-valued function
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,

\mathcal{F}[f]:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}
denotes its Fourier transform

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[f](x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(u)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}u^{\mathsf{T}}x}\mathrm{d}u=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(u_{1},u_{2})\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}(u_{1}x_{1}+u_{2}x_{2})}\mathrm{d}u_{1}\mathrm{d}u_{2}.

Since

\displaystyle\int_{{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}}\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}(u_{1}y+u_{2}z)\Delta_{N}^{-1/2}}\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=\int_{a_{N}^{({k_{1}}-1)}}^{a_{N}^{(k_{1})}}\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}u_{1}y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2}}\mathrm{d}u_{1}\int_{a_{N}^{({k_{2}}-1)}}^{a_{N}^{(k_{2})}}\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}u_{2}z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2}}\mathrm{d}u_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta_{N}}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}yz}\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}a_{N}^{(k_{1})}y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2}}-\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}a_{N}^{(k_{1}-1)}y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2}}\bigr)\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}a_{N}^{(k_{2})}z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2}}-\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}a_{N}^{(k_{2}-1)}z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta_{N}}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}yz}(\mathrm{e}^{\pi\mathrm{i}y}-\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\mathrm{i}y})(\mathrm{e}^{\pi\mathrm{i}z}-\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\mathrm{i}z})\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}(k_{1}y+k_{2}z)}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{N}\frac{\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}{\pi^{2}yz}\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}(k_{1}y+k_{2}z)},

it follows that

\displaystyle\Delta_{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\cos(2\pi k_{1}y)\cos(2\pi k_{2}z)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta_{N}}{2}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\bigl(\cos(2\pi(k_{1}y+k_{2}z))+\cos(2\pi(k_{1}y-k_{2}z))\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Re}\biggl(\frac{\Delta_{N}}{2}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}(k_{1}y+k_{2}z)}+\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}(k_{1}y-k_{2}z)}\bigr)\biggr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Re}\biggl(\frac{\pi^{2}yz}{2\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\int_{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}(u_{1}y+u_{2}z)\Delta_{N}^{-1/2}}+\mathrm{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}(u_{1}y-u_{2}z)\Delta_{N}^{-1/2}}\bigr)\mathrm{d}u\biggr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Re}\biggl(\frac{\pi^{2}yz}{2\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\mathcal{F}\biggl[\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\boldsymbol{1}_{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}\bigg](-2\pi y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2},-2\pi z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathrm{Re}\biggl(\frac{\pi^{2}yz}{2\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\mathcal{F}\biggl[\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\boldsymbol{1}_{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}\bigg](-2\pi y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2},2\pi z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\biggr)

(5.10)

\displaystyle=T_{1}+T_{2}-T_{3},

where

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Re}\biggl(\frac{\pi^{2}yz}{2\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\mathcal{F}\biggl[\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\boldsymbol{1}_{{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}}-g\boldsymbol{1}_{E_{N}}\bigg](-2\pi y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2},-2\pi z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathrm{Re}\biggl(\frac{\pi^{2}yz}{2\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\mathcal{F}\biggl[\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\boldsymbol{1}_{{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}}-g\boldsymbol{1}_{E_{N}}\bigg](-2\pi y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2},2\pi z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\biggr),

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Re}\biggl(\frac{\pi^{2}yz}{2\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\mathcal{F}[g\boldsymbol{1}_{E_{N}\cup D_{N}^{(1)}}](-2\pi y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2},-2\pi z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathrm{Re}\biggl(\frac{\pi^{2}yz}{2\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\mathcal{F}[g\boldsymbol{1}_{E_{N}\cup D_{N}^{(1)}}](-2\pi y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2},2\pi z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\biggr),

\displaystyle T_{3}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Re}\biggl(\frac{\pi^{2}yz}{2\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\mathcal{F}[g\boldsymbol{1}_{D_{N}^{(1)}}](-2\pi y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2},-2\pi z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathrm{Re}\biggl(\frac{\pi^{2}yz}{2\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\mathcal{F}[g\boldsymbol{1}_{D_{N}^{(1)}}](-2\pi y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2},2\pi z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\biggr).

As proved below,
T_{1}
,
T_{2}
, and
T_{3}
can be evaluated as

(5.11)

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle=O\bigl(\delta^{-3}(R_{1,N}\lor R_{2,N})\bigr),

(5.12)

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=O(\delta^{-3}R_{1,N}),

(5.13)

\displaystyle T_{3}

\displaystyle=O\bigl(\delta^{-2}(\Delta_{N}^{1-\beta/2}\lor R_{1,N})\bigr),

and we obtain the desired result from (
5.1
) and (
5.10
).

Proof of (
5.12
).

Since

\displaystyle x_{1}^{2}\mathcal{F}[g\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathbb{R}\times(a_{N}^{(0)},\infty)}](x_{1},x_{2})=(-\mathrm{i})^{2}\mathcal{F}[\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}g\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathbb{R}\times(a_{N}^{(0)},\infty)}](x_{1},x_{2}),

it holds that

\displaystyle|x_{1}^{2}\mathcal{F}[g\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathbb{R}\times(a_{N}^{(0)},\infty)}](x_{1},x_{2})|

\displaystyle=|\mathcal{F}[\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}g\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathbb{R}\times(a_{N}^{(0)},\infty)}](x_{1},x_{2})|\leq\|\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}g\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathbb{R}\times(a_{N}^{(0)},\infty)}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

that is, for
x_{1}\neq 0
,

(5.14)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{F}[g\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathbb{R}\times(a_{N}^{(0)},\infty)}](x_{1},x_{2})|\leq|x_{1}|^{-2}\|\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}g\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathbb{R}\times(a_{N}^{(0)},\infty)}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Let

G_{N}^{(1)}=E_{N}\cup D_{N}^{(1)}=(0,\infty)\times(a_{N}^{(0)},\infty)

and

G_{N}^{(2)}=(-\infty,0]\times(a_{N}^{(0)},\infty)
.
From

\displaystyle\mathrm{Re}\bigl(\mathcal{F}[g\boldsymbol{1}_{G_{N}^{(2)}}](x_{1},x_{2})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\int_{G_{N}^{(2)}}g(u)\cos(u_{1}x_{1}+u_{2}x_{2})\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=\int_{G_{N}^{(1)}}g(u)\cos(u_{1}x_{1}-u_{2}x_{2})\mathrm{d}u=\mathrm{Re}\bigl(\mathcal{F}[g\boldsymbol{1}_{G_{N}^{(1)}}](x_{1},-x_{2})\bigr),

we see that

(5.15)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Re}\bigl(\mathcal{F}[g\boldsymbol{1}_{E_{N}\cup D_{N}^{(1)}}](x_{1},x_{2})\bigr)+\mathrm{Re}\bigl(\mathcal{F}[g\boldsymbol{1}_{E_{N}\cup D_{N}^{(1)}}](x_{1},-x_{2})\bigr)=\mathrm{Re}\bigl(\mathcal{F}[g\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathbb{R}\times(a_{N}^{(0)},\infty)}](x_{1},x_{2})\bigr).

Since
1/\sin(\pi y)\leq\delta^{-1}
,
y\in[\delta,1-\delta]
and

\Delta_{N}\|\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}g\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathbb{R}\times(a_{N}^{(0)},\infty)}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=O(R_{1,N})
,
it follows from (
5.14
) and (
5.15
) that

\displaystyle|T_{2}|

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{2}yz}{2\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\bigl|\mathrm{Re}\bigl(\mathcal{F}[g\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathbb{R}\times(a_{N}^{(0)},\infty)}](-2\pi y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2},-2\pi z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\bigr)\bigr|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\pi z\Delta_{N}}{\pi y\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\|\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}g\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathbb{R}\times(a_{N}^{(0)},\infty)}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

(5.16)

\displaystyle=O(\delta^{-3}R_{1,N}).

Proof of (
5.13
).

Applying integration by parts to
T_{3}
, we obtain that

\displaystyle T_{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{2}yz}{\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\int_{D_{N}^{(2)}}g(x)\cos(2\pi x_{1}y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\cos(2\pi x_{2}z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{2}yz}{\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\int_{a_{N}^{(0)}}^{\infty}\cos(2\pi x_{2}z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\int_{0}^{a_{N}^{(0)}}g(x_{1},x_{2})\cos(2\pi x_{1}y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\mathrm{d}x_{1}\mathrm{d}x_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi z\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}{2\sin(\pi z)}\int_{a_{N}^{(0)}}^{\infty}g(a_{N}^{(0)},x_{2})\cos(2\pi x_{2}z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\mathrm{d}x_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{\pi^{2}yz\Delta_{N}}{\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\int_{a_{N}^{(0)}}^{\infty}\cos(2\pi x_{2}z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\int_{0}^{1/2}\partial_{x_{1}}g(x_{1}\Delta_{N}^{1/2},x_{2})\frac{\sin(2\pi x_{1}y)}{2\pi y}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\mathrm{d}x_{2}

(5.17)

\displaystyle=:I_{1}-I_{2}.

Applying the integration by parts to
I_{1}
again, we see that

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi z\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}{2\sin(\pi z)}\int_{a_{N}^{(0)}}^{\infty}g(a_{N}^{(0)},x_{2})\cos(2\pi x_{2}z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\mathrm{d}x_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Delta_{N}}{4}g(a_{N}^{(0)},a_{N}^{(0)})-\frac{\pi z\Delta_{N}}{2\sin(\pi z)}\int_{a_{N}^{(0)}}^{\infty}\partial_{x_{2}}g(a_{N}^{(0)},x_{2})\frac{\sin(2\pi x_{2}z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})}{2\pi z}\mathrm{d}x_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Delta_{N}}{4}g(a_{N}^{(0)},a_{N}^{(0)})-\frac{\Delta_{N}}{4\sin(\pi z)}\int_{a_{N}^{(0)}}^{\infty}\partial_{x_{2}}g(a_{N}^{(0)},x_{2})\sin(2\pi x_{2}z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\mathrm{d}x_{2}.

Noting that

(5.18)

|\partial_{x_{1}}g(x_{1},x_{2})|\lesssim|x_{1}||f^{\prime}(\theta_{2}\pi^{2}|x|^{2})|,

-\frac{\Delta_{N}}{4}g(a_{N}^{(0)},a_{N}^{(0)})=O(\Delta_{N}f(\Delta_{N}))=O(\Delta_{N}^{1-\beta/2})

and

\displaystyle\Biggl|\frac{\Delta_{N}}{4\sin(\pi z)}\int_{a_{N}^{(0)}}^{\infty}\partial_{x_{2}}g(a_{N}^{(0)},x_{2})\sin(2\pi x_{2}z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\mathrm{d}x_{2}\Biggr|

\displaystyle=O\biggl(\delta^{-1}\Delta_{N}\int_{\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}^{\infty}s|f^{\prime}(s^{2})|\mathrm{d}s\biggr)=O(\delta^{-1}R_{1,N}),

we have
I_{1}=O(\Delta_{N}^{1-\beta/2}\lor\delta^{-1}R_{1,N})
.
Furthermore, since
I_{2}
can be evaluated as

\displaystyle|I_{2}|

\displaystyle=\Biggl|\frac{\pi^{2}yz\Delta_{N}}{\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\int_{a_{N}^{(0)}}^{\infty}\cos(2\pi x_{2}z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\int_{0}^{1/2}\partial_{x_{1}}g(x_{1}\Delta_{N}^{1/2},x_{2})\frac{\sin(2\pi x_{1}y)}{2\pi y}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\mathrm{d}x_{2}\Biggr|

\displaystyle=O\biggl(\delta^{-2}\Delta_{N}\int_{a_{N}^{(0)}}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{1/2}|\partial_{x_{1}}g(x_{1}\Delta_{N}^{1/2},x_{2})|\mathrm{d}x_{1}\mathrm{d}x_{2}\biggr)

\displaystyle=O\biggl(\delta^{-2}\Delta_{N}\int_{D_{N}^{(2)}}|\partial_{x_{1}}g(x)|\mathrm{d}x\biggr)=O(\delta^{-2}R_{1,N}),

we obtain

T_{3}=O(\delta^{-2}(\Delta_{N}^{1-\beta/2}\lor R_{1,N}))
.

Proof of (
5.11
).

Since

\displaystyle\mathrm{Re}(\mathcal{F}[g](y,z)+\mathcal{F}[g](y,-z))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Re}\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}g(x)(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}(x_{1}y+x_{2}z)}+\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}(x_{1}y-x_{2}z)})\mathrm{d}x\biggr)

\displaystyle=2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}g(x)\cos(x_{1}y)\cos(x_{2}z)\mathrm{d}x,

T_{1}
can be evaluated as follows.

\displaystyle|T_{1}|

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{2}yz}{\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\Biggl|\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\int_{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}\bigl(g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})-g(x)\bigr)\cos(2\pi x_{1}y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\cos(2\pi x_{2}z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\mathrm{d}x\Biggr|

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{2}yz}{\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\Biggl|\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\int_{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}\biggl(\partial g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})(x-\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{2}(x-\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})^{\mathsf{T}}\int_{0}^{1}(1-u)\partial^{2}g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2}+u(x-\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2}))\mathrm{d}u(x-\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\cos(2\pi x_{1}y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\cos(2\pi x_{2}z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\mathrm{d}x\Biggr|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\pi^{2}yz}{\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\Biggl|\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\int_{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}\partial g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})(x-\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\cos(2\pi x_{1}y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\cos(2\pi x_{2}z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\mathrm{d}x\Biggr|

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\pi^{2}yz}{2\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\int_{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}\biggl|\int_{0}^{1}(1-u)\partial^{2}g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2}+u(x-\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2}))\mathrm{d}u\biggr||x-\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2}|^{2}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{2}yz}{\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\Biggl|\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\partial g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\int_{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}(x-\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\cos(2\pi x_{1}y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\cos(2\pi x_{2}z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\mathrm{d}x\Biggr|

(5.19)

\displaystyle\qquad+O\biggl(\delta^{-2}\Delta_{N}\int_{E_{N}}|\partial^{2}g(x)|\mathrm{d}x\biggr).

Since

\displaystyle\int_{a_{N}^{(k_{1}-1)}}^{a_{N}^{(k_{1})}}(x_{1}-k_{1}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\cos(2\pi x_{1}y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\mathrm{d}x_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta_{N}}{2\pi^{2}y^{2}}\sin(2\pi k_{1}y)\bigl(\pi y\cos(\pi y)-\sin(\pi y)\bigr),

\displaystyle\int_{a_{N}^{(k_{2}-1)}}^{a_{N}^{(k_{2})}}\cos(2\pi x_{2}z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\mathrm{d}x_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}{2\pi z}\Bigl(\sin\bigl(\pi z(2k_{2}+1)\bigr)-\sin\bigl(\pi z(2k_{2}-1)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\Delta_{N}^{1/2}\frac{\sin(\pi z)}{\pi z}\cos(2\pi k_{2}z),

the integral of the first term of (
5.19
) can be expressed as

\displaystyle\int_{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}(x-\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\cos(2\pi x_{1}y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\cos(2\pi x_{2}z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\displaystyle\int_{a_{N}^{(k_{1}-1)}}^{a_{N}^{(k_{1})}}(x_{1}-k_{1}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\cos(2\pi x_{1}y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\mathrm{d}x_{1}\int_{a_{N}^{(k_{2}-1)}}^{a_{N}^{(k_{2})}}\cos(2\pi x_{2}z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\mathrm{d}x_{2}\\
\displaystyle\int_{a_{N}^{(k_{1}-1)}}^{a_{N}^{(k_{1})}}\cos(2\pi x_{1}y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\mathrm{d}x_{1}\int_{a_{N}^{(k_{2}-1)}}^{a_{N}^{(k_{2})}}(x_{2}-k_{2}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\cos(2\pi x_{2}z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\mathrm{d}x_{2}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{N}^{3/2}\begin{pmatrix}\displaystyle\sin(2\pi k_{1}y)\cos(2\pi k_{2}z)\frac{\pi y\cos(\pi y)-\sin(\pi y)}{2\pi^{2}y^{2}}\frac{\sin(\pi z)}{\pi z}\\
\displaystyle\cos(2\pi k_{1}y)\sin(2\pi k_{2}z)\frac{\sin(\pi y)}{\pi y}\frac{\pi z\cos(\pi z)-\sin(\pi z)}{2\pi^{2}z^{2}}\end{pmatrix}.

Since
(\pi y\cos(\pi y)-\sin(\pi y))/2\pi^{2}y^{2}
is bounded in
y\in[0,1]
,
the first term of (
5.19
) can be evaluated as follows.

\displaystyle\frac{\pi^{2}yz}{\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\Biggl|\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\partial g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\int_{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}(x-\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\cos(2\pi x_{1}y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\cos(2\pi x_{2}z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\mathrm{d}x\Biggr|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\pi y}{\sin(\pi y)}\frac{\pi y\cos(\pi y)-\sin(\pi y)}{2\pi^{2}y^{2}}\Biggl|\Delta_{N}^{3/2}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\partial g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\sin(2\pi k_{1}y)\cos(2\pi k_{2}z)\Biggr|

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\pi z}{\sin(\pi z)}\frac{\pi z\cos(\pi z)-\sin(\pi z)}{2\pi^{2}z^{2}}\Biggl|\Delta_{N}^{3/2}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\partial g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\cos(2\pi k_{1}y)\sin(2\pi k_{2}z)\Biggr|

\displaystyle=O\Biggl(\delta^{-1}\Delta_{N}^{3/2}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\partial g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\sin(2\pi k_{1}y)\cos(2\pi k_{2}z)\Biggr).

According to

(5.20)

\displaystyle\Delta_{N}^{3/2}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\partial g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\sin(2\pi k_{1}y)\cos(2\pi k_{2}z)=O\bigl(\delta^{-2}(R_{1,N}\lor R_{2,N})\bigr),

the first term of (
5.19
) is

O(\delta^{-3}(R_{1,N}\lor R_{2,N}))
,
and thus we obtain (
5.11
).
Next, we will show (
5.20
).

Proof of (
5.20
).

In a similar way to (
5.10
), it follows that

\displaystyle\Delta_{N}^{3/2}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\partial g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\sin(2\pi k_{1}y)\cos(2\pi k_{2}z)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta_{N}^{3/2}}{2}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\partial g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\bigl(\sin(2\pi(k_{1}y+k_{2}z))+\sin(2\pi(k_{1}y-k_{2}z))\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Im}\biggl(\frac{\pi^{2}yz\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}{2\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\mathcal{F}\biggl[\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\partial g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\boldsymbol{1}_{{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}}\biggl](-2\pi y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2},-2\pi z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathrm{Im}\biggl(\frac{\pi^{2}yz\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}{2\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\mathcal{F}\biggl[\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\partial g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\boldsymbol{1}_{{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}}\biggl](-2\pi y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2},2\pi z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\biggr)

(5.21)

\displaystyle=:S_{1}+S_{2}-S_{3},

where

\displaystyle S_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Im}\biggl(\frac{\pi^{2}yz\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}{2\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\mathcal{F}\biggl[\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\partial g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\boldsymbol{1}_{{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}}-\partial g\boldsymbol{1}_{E_{N}}\biggl](-2\pi y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2},-2\pi y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathrm{Im}\biggl(\frac{\pi^{2}yz\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}{2\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\mathcal{F}\biggl[\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\partial g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\boldsymbol{1}_{{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}}-\partial g\boldsymbol{1}_{E_{N}}\biggl](-2\pi y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2},2\pi y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\biggr),

\displaystyle S_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Im}\biggl(\frac{\pi^{2}yz\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}{2\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\mathcal{F}[\partial g\boldsymbol{1}_{E_{N}\cup D_{N}^{(1)}}](-2\pi y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2},-2\pi z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathrm{Im}\biggl(\frac{\pi^{2}yz\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}{2\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\mathcal{F}[\partial g\boldsymbol{1}_{E_{N}\cup D_{N}^{(1)}}](-2\pi y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2},2\pi z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\biggr),

\displaystyle S_{3}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Im}\biggl(\frac{\pi^{2}yz\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}{2\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\mathcal{F}[\partial g\boldsymbol{1}_{D_{N}^{(1)}}](-2\pi y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2},-2\pi z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathrm{Im}\biggl(\frac{\pi^{2}yz\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}{2\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\mathcal{F}[\partial g\boldsymbol{1}_{D_{N}^{(1)}}](-2\pi y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2},2\pi z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\biggr).

Since

(5.22)

\displaystyle|S_{1}|\leq\frac{\pi^{2}yz\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}{\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\biggl\|\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\partial g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\boldsymbol{1}_{{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}}-\partial g\boldsymbol{1}_{E_{N}}\biggr\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

\pi y/\sin(\pi y)\leq\delta^{-1}
,
y\in[\delta,1-\delta]
and

\displaystyle\Delta_{N}^{1/2}\biggl\|\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\partial g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\boldsymbol{1}_{{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}}-\partial g\boldsymbol{1}_{E_{N}}\biggr\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{N}^{1/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}\biggl|\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\partial g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\boldsymbol{1}_{{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}}(x)-\partial g(x)\boldsymbol{1}_{E}(x)\biggr|\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\Delta_{N}^{1/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\bigl|\partial g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})-\partial g(x)\bigr|\boldsymbol{1}_{{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}}(x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\Delta_{N}^{1/2}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\int_{{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}}\bigl|\partial g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})-\partial g(x)\bigr|\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\Delta_{N}^{1/2}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\int_{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}\biggl\|\int_{0}^{1}\partial^{2}g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2}+u(x-\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2}))\mathrm{d}u\biggr\||x-\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2}|\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=O\biggl(\Delta_{N}\int_{E_{N}}\|\partial^{2}g(x)\|\mathrm{d}x\biggr)=O(R_{1,N}),

we have
|S_{1}|=O(\delta^{-2}R_{1,N})
.
Noting that for
x_{1}\neq 0
,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{F}[\partial g\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathbb{R}\times(a_{N}^{(0)},\infty)}](x_{1},x_{2})|\leq|x_{1}|^{-1}\|\partial_{x_{1}}\partial g\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathbb{R}\times(a_{N}^{(0)},\infty)}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

we see that

(5.23)

\displaystyle|S_{2}|\leq\frac{\pi z\Delta_{N}}{\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\|\partial_{x_{1}}\partial g\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathbb{R}\times(a_{N}^{(0)},\infty)}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=O(\delta^{-2}R_{1,N}).

Moreover, it follows from (
5.18
) that

\displaystyle S_{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{2}yz\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}{\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\int_{D_{N}^{(1)}}\partial g(x)\sin(2\pi x_{1}y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\cos(2\pi x_{2}z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{2}yz\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}{\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\int_{a_{N}^{(0)}}^{\infty}\cos(2\pi x_{2}z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\int_{0}^{a_{N}^{(0)}}\partial g(x_{1},x_{2})\sin(2\pi x_{1}y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\mathrm{d}x_{1}\mathrm{d}x_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi z\Delta_{N}}{2\sin(\pi y)\sin(\pi z)}\biggl(\int_{a_{N}^{(0)}}^{\infty}(\partial g(0,x_{2})-\partial g(a_{N}^{(0)},x_{2})\cos(\pi y))\cos(2\pi x_{2}z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\mathrm{d}x_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\int_{a_{N}^{(0)}}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{a_{N}^{(0)}}\partial_{x_{1}}\partial g(x)\cos(2\pi x_{1}y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\cos(2\pi x_{2}z\Delta_{N}^{-1/2})\mathrm{d}x_{1}\mathrm{d}x_{2}\biggr)

\displaystyle=O\Biggl(\delta^{-2}\Delta_{N}\biggl(\int_{\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}^{\infty}s|f^{\prime}(s^{2})|\mathrm{d}s\lor\int_{D_{N}^{(1)}}\|\partial^{2}g(x)\|\mathrm{d}x\biggr)\lor\delta^{-2}\Delta_{N}^{3/2}\int_{\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}^{\infty}|f^{\prime}(s^{2})|\mathrm{d}s\Biggr)

(5.24)

\displaystyle=O\bigl(\delta^{-2}(R_{1,N}\lor R_{2,N})\bigr).

Therefore, this completes the proof of (
5.20
).

(2) In the same way as (
5.5
), we have

(5.25)

\displaystyle\Delta_{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}f(\lambda_{\boldsymbol{k}}\Delta_{N})\cos(2\pi k_{1}y)=\Delta_{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\cos(2\pi k_{1}y)+O(R_{1,N}).

Note that the first term on the right hand side of (
5.25
)
is obtained by substituting
0
for
z
for (
5.10
).
Thus,
setting
\pi z/\sin(\pi z)=1
if
z=0
, we have

\displaystyle\Delta_{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\cos(2\pi k_{1}y)=T_{1}+T_{2}-T_{3},

where

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Re}\biggl(\frac{\pi y}{\sin(\pi y)}\mathcal{F}\biggl[\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\boldsymbol{1}_{{E_{N}^{(\boldsymbol{k})}}}-g\boldsymbol{1}_{E_{N}}\biggl](-2\pi y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2},0)\biggr),

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Re}\biggl(\frac{\pi y}{\sin(\pi y)}\mathcal{F}[g\boldsymbol{1}_{E_{N}\cup D_{N}^{(1)}}](-2\pi y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2},0)\biggr),

\displaystyle T_{3}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Re}\biggl(\frac{\pi y}{\sin(\pi y)}\mathcal{F}[g\boldsymbol{1}_{D_{N}^{(1)}}](-2\pi y\Delta_{N}^{-1/2},0)\biggr).

From (
5.21
)-(
5.24
), it holds that

\displaystyle\Delta_{N}^{3/2}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\partial g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\sin(2\pi k_{1}y)=O\bigl(\delta^{-1}(R_{1,N}\lor R_{2,N})\bigr).

Setting
(\pi z\cos(\pi z)-\sin(\pi z))/2\pi^{2}z^{2}=0
if
z=0
, we see that

\displaystyle|T_{1}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\pi y}{\sin(\pi y)}\frac{\pi y\cos(\pi y)-\sin(\pi y)}{2\pi^{2}y^{2}}\Biggl|\Delta_{N}^{3/2}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\partial g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\sin(2\pi k_{1}y)\Biggr|

\displaystyle=O\Biggl(\delta^{-1}\Delta_{N}^{3/2}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}\partial g(\boldsymbol{k}\Delta_{N}^{1/2})\sin(2\pi k_{1}y)\Biggr)

\displaystyle=O\bigl(\delta^{-2}(R_{1,N}\lor R_{2,N})\bigr).

Furthermore, we obtain from (
5.16
) and (
5.17
) that

T_{2}=O(\delta^{-2}R_{1,N})
and

T_{3}=I_{1}-I_{2}
, where

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta_{N}^{1/2}}{2}\int_{a_{N}^{(0)}}^{\infty}g(a_{N}^{(0)},x_{2})\mathrm{d}x_{2}=\int_{a_{N}^{(0)}}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{a_{N}^{(0)}}g(a_{N}^{(0)},x_{2})\mathrm{d}x_{1}\mathrm{d}x_{2},

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi y\Delta_{N}}{\sin(\pi y)}\int_{a_{N}^{(0)}}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{1/2}\partial_{x_{1}}g(x_{1}\Delta_{N}^{1/2},x_{2})\frac{\sin(2\pi x_{1}y)}{2\pi y}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\mathrm{d}x_{2}.

Noting that (
5.18
) and
